# BEFORE on Astra, read as a story

Runs `site/runs_extra/astra_json_prev_orig` (hops 1 to 150) and `astra_json_prev_orig_ext` (151 to 300, continued from hop 150's exact output), 2026-09-06. GPT-6 Astra, effort low, default sampling. Rule: an original passage of about 250 words that comes immediately BEFORE the current text in the same story. Each passage was written seeing only the one after it, so the story reads in reverse hop order: hop 300 is the earliest moment, hop 1 is the passage just before the seed, and the seed sentence is the end. Nothing is edited; passages are the `text` fields verbatim, with the hop number they were generated at.

---

**hop 300**

Janet arrived on Sunday with a cake in a tin they recognised. Elsie had given her the tin at Christmas, full of biscuits, and now neither of them mentioned whether it was being returned.

“I’ll get a plate,” Elsie said.

He took Janet’s coat and hung it over his own. She kept her cardigan on while they stood in the hall.

“Traffic all right?”

“Not bad. A bit coming off the bypass.”

He nodded, although he had not used the bypass since they changed the roundabout.

In the sitting room, Janet looked towards the window. They had moved the table there to give the radiator room, and its old place showed on the wallpaper.

“You’ve had a shift round.”

“Your mother wanted more light.”

“It’s better for reading,” Elsie said from the doorway.

He went upstairs for the spare chair. Coming down, he rested it on the landing while he moved a pair of shoes with his foot. Janet offered to help, but by then he had it through the door.

They ate the cake with small forks. Janet told them about a woman at work whose son had gone to Canada. Elsie asked whether he had found somewhere to live.

Later, while Janet was in the kitchen, he noticed her cardigan laid across the sofa arm. He moved it to make room for the tin.

“Will you take the rest?” Elsie asked when Janet was leaving.

“Keep it for tomorrow.”

After the car had gone, they watched the news. The chair remained between them and the television, slightly to one side.

---

**hop 299**

On Monday morning he took the spare chair back upstairs. Janet had sat on it on Sunday, though there had been room for her on the sofa. She said it was easier on her back.

“Mind the paint,” Elsie called from the bottom.

He turned the chair sideways at the bend. One leg caught the banister, making a small sound that seemed louder because he had been trying not to make it.

“Have you marked it?”

“No.”

He rubbed the place with his thumb before going on.

In the little bedroom, the bed was covered with things they had not yet decided to put away: a shopping bag of photographs, Janet’s old sewing machine, three curtains folded with their linings out. He set the chair beside the wardrobe. There was a square of carpet beneath it that still held the marks from where it had stood before.

When he came down, Elsie was rinsing the cups they had used at breakfast.

“She left her cardigan.”

“Who did?”

Elsie looked at him.

“I’ll ring her,” he said.

“She knows. She rang about it.”

He dried the cups and put them in the cupboard, then took one down again for the tea he had forgotten was brewing.

They spent the afternoon in the garden. Elsie cut back the lavender while he held the sack open. Afterwards he stayed outside to sweep the path. Through the kitchen window he could see her standing at the sink, washing the scissors. She held them up to the light, opened them, and washed them again.

**hop 298**

The kitchen clock had stopped at twenty past four. For two days neither of them mentioned it. Then, on Wednesday, Elsie asked whether he was going to leave it like that.

“I thought you liked it quiet.”

“Not knowing the time isn’t the same as quiet.”

He lifted it off its hook. Behind it was a pale circle and, below that, a little crescent of grease the cloth had never reached.

Elsie watched him put the clock face down on the table.

“Put something under it.”

He used the newspaper, although he had not finished the crossword. The battery had leaked at one end. He scraped the white deposit away with a knife while Elsie stood beside him holding a folded piece of kitchen roll.

“Not that knife,” she said.

He looked at it. It was the one with the loose handle, which neither of them used for eating.

When he had finished, he wrapped the battery in the kitchen roll and put it by the bread bin.

“That’ll get thrown away,” Elsie said.

“It’s finished.”

“They have a place for them at the library.”

He moved it into his coat pocket. There was already one there, smaller, and a receipt whose print had gone grey.

They went out after lunch but forgot the library. At the crossing Elsie took his sleeve, then let go when the traffic stopped. They bought potatoes, two chops and a packet of biscuits Janet liked.

Back home, the clock was still on the table. He put it on the dresser so they could eat.

**hop 297**

On Thursday he found a key in the drawer where they kept the batteries. It was tied to a luggage label, but whatever had been written on the label had faded to a faint blue line.

“Do you know what this opens?”

Elsie was sitting by the back door, taking the brown leaves off a geranium. She held out her hand without looking up.

He put the key in it.

“Not this house,” she said.

“I can see that.”

She rubbed the teeth with her thumb, then gave it back.

“Perhaps your mother’s.”

His mother’s house had been sold in 1986. He remembered signing something in an office above a building society. Afterwards they had bought sandwiches and eaten them in the car because Elsie hadn’t wanted to pay for tea.

“There were two keys,” he said.

“Well, there would have been.”

He left it on the kitchen windowsill. Later, washing a cup, he noticed that Elsie had moved it onto a saucer.

That evening they watched a programme about a railway neither of them had travelled on. Elsie said the stations all looked the same now. He thought she meant they looked like one another, but she meant they had not changed.

Before bed he checked the back door. The geranium stood against the glass, its remaining leaves turned towards the room.

“Janet usually rings on Thursdays,” Elsie said from the stairs.

“She rang on Sunday.”

“That was about the boiler.”

He switched off the kitchen light and followed her up.

**hop 296**

Janet rang on Friday while he was scraping the last of the marmalade into a smaller jar. Elsie took the telephone into the hall, although there was no one else to disturb.

He could hear her saying that they were both quite well. Then she said it again, with a little more space between the words.

When she came back, he held up the jar.

“Enough for tomorrow.”

“She’s doing something for the children. About the family.”

“A tree?”

“I don’t think you call it that now.”

He screwed the lid on and wiped the knife against a piece of toast.

Janet wanted photographs, Elsie said. Early ones, if they had any. There was one of his father standing beside a motorbike, though neither of them could remember whether the motorbike had belonged to him.

“Borrowed,” he said.

“Was it?”

“He never had the money.”

After lunch Elsie brought down the shoe box from the spare room. A Christmas ribbon had caught beneath the lid. She pulled it free and wound it around two fingers before putting it in her cardigan pocket.

They looked through the top few photographs. In one, Janet sat on a wall holding a paper bag. Behind her was the sea, or possibly a stretch of pale sky.

“That was the year it rained,” Elsie said.

He turned it over. There was nothing on the back.

They left the box on the dining table overnight. Before drawing the curtains, Elsie moved it away from the window, where the morning sun would come in.

**hop 295**

On Saturday Elsie found a packet of photograph envelopes in the sideboard. They had come free with something, years ago, and the gum on the flaps had gone yellow.

“Still useful,” she said.

He was kneeling by the television, looking for the socket that belonged to the lamp.

“Is this one doing anything?”

“The lamp.”

He put it back. The bulb had gone. He had known that before getting down, but once he was there it seemed worth checking.

Elsie laid the envelopes on the table and flattened them with a cookbook. Then she lifted the cookbook to see whether it had worked.

“Janet might like some,” she said.

“Envelopes?”

“Photographs.”

“She’s got photographs.”

“Not these.”

He stood, holding the edge of the television cabinet. A pale rectangle showed on the wallpaper where they had taken the clock down. He had meant to move the small landscape across, but its wire was too short for the hook.

In the kitchen he filled the kettle. After he closed the tap, a drop gathered underneath it and fell onto a plate.

“That wants a washer,” he said.

“There are some in the garage.”

“I know.”

He turned the handle harder. For a little while nothing happened.

They had their tea in the sitting room. Elsie put her cup on an old electricity bill rather than reach for a coaster. Before bed he carried the cups through and moved the plate out of the sink. The drops were louder without it, so he put it back.

From upstairs Elsie called to ask whether he had locked the door.

---

**hop 294**

On Sunday he went into the garage for a washer and came back with a tin of picture hooks. Elsie was at the kitchen table, taking the paper sleeves off some photographs.

“Was it dripping?” she asked.

“Only when you turn it off.”

She looked up at him.

“I’ll do it tomorrow,” he said.

He put the tin beside the bread bin. There were washers in it once, he was almost certain. He shook a few hooks into his palm and found a small brass key.

“That’s the clock,” Elsie said.

“We haven’t got the clock.”

“No.”

He put it back with the hooks.

The photographs had been kept in the drawer under the telephone, behind the takeaway menus. Elsie made two piles, then moved one photograph from the larger pile to the smaller. He could see Janet standing beside a horse. Someone’s thumb covered the bottom corner.

“Her first lesson,” he said.

“Her last. She’d had the fall by then.”

He remembered waiting in the car with the heater on. There had been mud on the back of his seat for weeks afterwards.

Elsie slipped the photograph into a fresh envelope.

“Did she ring?” he asked.

“While you were out there.”

“What did she want?”

“Nothing. To see how we were.”

At tea he fetched the good marmalade because the other jar was empty. Elsie scraped the last of it onto her toast anyway. Afterwards she washed both jars and stood them upside down beside the sink. He dried the spoons and waited for her to move so he could put them away.

**hop 293**

On Monday Elsie asked him to bring the steps upstairs. He found them behind the mower, with a length of string wound round one leg. By the time he had untied it she was calling down to ask whether he had found them.

“The little ones?” he said.

“The ones we’ve got.”

She wanted the box from the top of the wardrobe. He stood on the second step while she held the side, though it was steady enough. There was dust on the lid. He wiped it with his sleeve before handing it down.

Inside were two curtains and Janet’s riding hat. Elsie put the hat on the bed.

“She might want that.”

“It won’t fit her now.”

“Not to wear.”

He folded the curtains along their old creases. One had a hook still attached, and he worked it loose with his thumbnail.

“Are we doing the other room?” he asked.

“Not today.”

He left the steps on the landing. Later, going to bed, he caught his slipper against them and moved them into Janet’s room.

In the morning Elsie emptied his bottom drawer onto the bedspread. He watched her shake out a cardigan he had forgotten owning.

“There’s nothing wrong with that,” he said.

She held it towards him. He took it, then laid it on the chair.

The bag had come through the letterbox with a leaflet showing a child beside a hospital bed. Elsie read the collection date and smoothed it flat.

“We can take it ourselves,” she said. “No point leaving it out in the weather.”

**hop 292**

The charity bag had been hanging on the kitchen door since Tuesday. Each time he went through, the buckle of a belt knocked against the wood.

“Is that going?” he asked.

“It’s in the bag.”

He had meant the bag itself, but he said nothing. He lifted it off the handle and put it beside the back door, where they would remember it.

On Thursday Janet rang while Elsie was having her bath. He told her they were managing. She asked about the garden and he looked through the window before answering, as though something might have happened to it.

“Your mother’s getting rid of a few things.”

“What things?”

“Clothes. Whatever she finds.”

Janet was quiet for a moment. Then she asked him not to let anything of hers go without checking.

Afterwards he wrote JANET on the back of an envelope. When Elsie came down, he gave her the message and she turned the envelope over.

“This wants paying.”

“I paid it Monday.”

“Then you can throw it away.”

On Friday it rained. He took the charity bag to the shop and returned with a saucer he thought would fit the blue cup. It was a little too small. Elsie tried the cup on it, lifted it, and tried again.

“You should have taken the cup with you.”

He put the saucer in the cupboard beneath the others. That evening she asked if the torch had batteries. He found it in the drawer and shone it against his palm.

“Yes,” he said.

“Good. We’ll want that tomorrow.”

---

**hop 291**

On Saturday Elsie asked him to bring a box down from the loft. She stood at the foot of the ladder while he looked among the suitcases and the Christmas things.

“Not that one. The one with the string.”

He could see three with string, but he knew better than to say so. He brought down the nearest and waited while she opened it on the landing.

Inside were curtains, folded with the hooks still in them. Elsie lifted a corner and rubbed the material between her fingers.

“These were good curtains.”

“Dining room,” he said.

“No. Janet’s.”

He remembered yellow curtains in Janet’s room, but perhaps those had come later. There had been a time when Elsie was always changing something and he came home to find a chair somewhere else, a patch of pale wallpaper where a picture had hung.

“Shall I take them back up?”

“Leave them for now.”

She went downstairs, keeping one hand flat against the wall. He carried the box into the little room because it was in the way on the landing. The string trailed behind him. He tucked it inside before putting the lid on.

At supper she asked whether they had carrots for tomorrow.

“Half a bag.”

“That’ll do for us.”

Later, while she watched the weather, he checked the vegetable drawer. There were five carrots, one soft at the thin end. He put that one underneath the others and shut the drawer.

Upstairs, Elsie had left the loft ladder down. He folded it away without calling to her.

---

**hop 290**

On Sunday their daughter rang while he was peeling carrots. He dried his hands on the tea towel and took the telephone into the hall, though the lead would have reached the kitchen.

“Is Mum there?”

“She’s having a rest.”

There was a pause in which he could hear children, or a television with children in it.

“Tell her I called.”

He said he would. Then she asked whether they needed anything, and he looked at the little table beneath the mirror, with its bowl of keys that no longer opened any of their doors.

“No,” he said. “We’re all right.”

Elsie came down before he had finished the carrots. She had changed her cardigan but was still wearing the blouse with the missing button at the wrist.

“Who was that?”

“Janet. She sends her love.”

“Did you ask about the car?”

He had forgotten there was something wrong with it.

“She sounded busy.”

Elsie took a carrot from the colander and examined the dark place he had left near the top. She found the small knife in the drawer and cut it away.

After dinner he carried the clean towels upstairs. The airing cupboard was full, although he could remember when they had kept all four winter blankets on the bottom shelf. He stood with the towels against his chest until Elsie called from below.

“Put them on the bed in the little room.”

He pushed the door open with his elbow. A cardboard box occupied the pillow end. He moved it a few inches and laid the towels beside it.

**hop 289**

The spare room had been hers for sewing, though neither of them called it that now. The machine stood under its plastic cover, and the narrow bed was where they put things that ought to have gone somewhere else.

On Monday he brought the washing in before it was dry. Elsie watched him from the back door.

“Another hour would have done it.”

“It looked like rain.”

She looked up. He did too, holding the basket against his stomach.

They spread the sheets over the dining chairs. At supper they sat at opposite ends of the table because there was no room along the sides. He had made too much potato and put the saucepan between them, on a folded newspaper.

“You could freeze that,” she said.

He fitted the lid back on.

The next morning she went upstairs after breakfast and did not come down when the post arrived. There was a bill, a leaflet about gutters and a letter for the people who had lived there before them, nearly twenty years ago.

He carried the letter upstairs.

“Still getting these,” he said.

Elsie was sitting on the edge of the bed with one slipper on. The other lay on its side beneath the dressing table.

“Put not known.”

“I always do.”

“Well, do it again.”

He bent to retrieve the slipper and placed it beside her foot, facing the right way. Then he went down for a pen.

At the kitchen table he crossed out the address carefully, leaving the name untouched. He set the letter by the door, where he would see it when he went out.

---

**hop 288**

On Wednesday Elsie asked him to bring up a glass of water. He filled it at the kitchen tap and stood for a moment watching the bubbles clear.

“Not the heavy one,” she called down.

He poured it into another glass. Some went into the sink, and he topped it up before carrying it upstairs.

There was nowhere to set it beside her. The chair held her cardigan, two library books and the blue box in which she kept the tablets she was no longer taking. He moved the cardigan.

“That’ll fall through,” she said.

The chair seat was woven, with a hollow in the middle. He held the glass until she reached for it.

“You need something here.”

“I need you to pass me my glasses.”

They were on the windowsill behind the curtain. He fetched them and wiped one lens on his shirt, although she had not asked him to.

Later, in the kitchen, he looked through the catalogue that had come with the newspaper. There were beds with motors and chairs that tipped their occupants forward. A woman in pale trousers smiled beside a trolley carrying a single cup.

Elsie came in while he was folding it shut.

“Anything interesting?”

“Mostly shoes.”

She put the empty glass by the sink. He noticed she had used both hands to lower it, but when he offered tea she took the kettle from him.

That evening he went into the spare room for a clean pillowcase. He switched on the light, then stood in the doorway long enough for Elsie to ask what he had forgotten.

---

**hop 287**

On Thursday Arthur took the small table out of the spare room and measured it with the tape he kept in the biscuit tin. There were buttons in the tin too, though neither of them had put any there for years.

“What are you measuring?” Elsie called.

“Nothing yet.”

He wrote the width on the back of an envelope. Downstairs, he measured the space between the radiator and the sitting-room window, although that was not where he meant to put it.

At lunch he said, “We don’t use that table.”

“Which table?”

“The little one. With the drawer.”

“It’s got the Christmas paper in it.”

“That could go somewhere else.”

Elsie cut the crust from one end of her sandwich and left it beside her plate.

“It was your mother’s,” she said.

“I know whose it was.”

After lunch he opened the drawer. The paper had curled into a tube, trapping two flattened bows and a label with someone’s name crossed out. He shut it again without moving anything.

The table had a raised lip round the top. He had thought this would be useful, but now he noticed how low the legs were. From the bed, Elsie would have to reach down.

He returned the tape to the tin.

When he went upstairs that afternoon, she was resting with a book open on the blanket. Her glass stood on the floor.

“You’ll knock that over,” he said.

“I put it there to make room.”

She lifted the book, showing him the damp ring on its cover.

**hop 286**

The chair had been on Elsie’s side of the bed since they brought it up for her to sit on while she dressed. She no longer sat on it. A folded cardigan occupied the seat during the day, and at night she put her water there, beside the clock.

Arthur said the clock belonged on the chest of drawers.

“I can’t see it over there.”

“You can hear it.”

“That doesn’t tell me the time.”

On Friday he found a square of cork in the kitchen drawer and took it upstairs for the glass. It was a little too large for the flat part of the seat. One corner rose against the wooden curve at the back.

“It’ll wobble,” Elsie said.

He turned it round.

“It still will.”

He put the cork in his dressing-gown pocket. Later, looking for a tissue, he found it there and laid it on his own bedside cabinet.

On Saturday they changed the sheets. Arthur pulled his corner down too far and Elsie waited, holding hers, until he noticed.

“This chair’s always in the way,” he said.

“Push it towards the wardrobe.”

“Then you won’t get the door open.”

“Only while we’re doing this.”

He moved it with the clock still on the seat. The glass was downstairs, standing by the sink with half an inch of water in it.

That evening Elsie brought it up again. She set it near the front edge and put the clock behind it, tilted towards the pillow.

Arthur watched her reach from the bed to check the alarm.

---

**hop 285**

On Sunday Elsie spilled her water reaching for the clock. Most of it went onto the chair, but some ran between the seat and its wooden frame and dripped onto Arthur’s slipper.

He fetched a cloth from the bathroom.

“Not that one,” she said. “That’s for the basin.”

“It’s only water.”

She took it from him and pressed it into the corner of the seat. He picked up the clock and stood holding it while she wiped underneath.

“You could put the glass on the floor.”

“Then I’d knock it over getting up.”

Arthur turned the clock round. The little door over the battery had come loose again. He pushed it with his thumb until it clicked.

Downstairs, after breakfast, Elsie sat by the window with the newspaper folded to the television page. Arthur moved the dining-room curtains back from the radiator. Behind one of them stood the small table they used for things that had to be kept but not looked at: envelopes, spare keys, a packet of picture hooks.

“What about this?” he said.

She looked over her glasses.

“What about it?”

“For your side.”

“That’s where the basket goes.”

He measured the tabletop with his hands, then kept them apart as he turned towards her.

“It wouldn’t take much room.”

Elsie went back to the newspaper. There was a programme she wanted to mark, but the pencil had rolled under the cushion.

“We’d have to move the chair,” she said.

Arthur let his hands fall. He pulled the curtain clear of the table and left it tucked behind the radiator pipe.

**hop 284**

On Monday Arthur brought the small table upstairs. He had emptied its drawer first, leaving the contents in a neat pile on the dining-room carpet.

“It won’t go there,” Elsie said.

He was standing with it against his thighs, waiting for her to move the laundry basket.

“It’s narrower than the chair.”

“The chair doesn’t have a drawer.”

He put the table down and opened the drawer. It came halfway out before striking the bed.

“That’s enough for what you need.”

Elsie lifted the basket onto the quilt. Underneath was one of his socks, flattened where a basket foot had rested on it. She handed it to him.

The lamp had belonged on the hall cupboard. Neither of them could remember why they had stopped using it there. Arthur carried it up after tea, with the flex looped over his wrist, and fitted the plug into the socket behind the bed.

“Hold this,” he said, giving her the shade while he tightened the ring beneath it.

She held it with both hands. There was a brown mark near the seam, which she turned towards the wall when he had finished.

On Tuesday she brought up the book she had been reading downstairs. Arthur noticed it on the new table beside a glass of water.

“Better than the chair,” he said.

She took off her watch and laid it on the book.

“I’ll want somewhere for the basket.”

He looked at the space beside the wardrobe. The chair was there now, with his trousers folded over its back.

“We’ll find somewhere,” he said.

---

**hop 283**

On Wednesday evening Elsie lost her place in the book when the lamp went out. She kept a finger between the pages and reached behind the shade.

“Leave it a minute,” Arthur said.

“I haven’t done anything.”

He put his newspaper on his knees. From his side of the bed he could see the bulb, a pale shape under the pleated shade. Elsie pressed the switch twice. The light returned, showing the dust along the top of the headboard.

“There’s nothing wrong with it now,” she said.

Arthur lifted his newspaper again, but she closed the book.

In the morning he asked whether they had another bulb. Elsie was cutting the last slice of bread in half to make it fit the toaster.

“In the drawer with the batteries.”

“That one’s too big.”

“Then we haven’t.”

He opened the drawer nevertheless. There were birthday candles in a paper bag and the instructions for the washing machine they had replaced. At the back lay a bulb wrapped in kitchen paper. He unwrapped it and held it by the metal end.

“Too big,” Elsie said.

He put it back carefully.

Later, upstairs, he tried the lamp without telling her. It lit, and stayed lit while he straightened the books beside it. He switched it off before going down.

That night Elsie read with the landing door open. The strip of light reached across the carpet but stopped short of the bed.

“You’ll hurt your eyes,” he said.

“I’m only finishing the page.”

He stood in the doorway until she asked him to move.

---

**hop 282**

On Thursday Arthur brought the little lamp downstairs and stood it beside the toaster. Elsie moved it to the other end of the counter before putting the kettle on.

“Is that where you’re doing it?” she asked.

“I only need to see the plug.”

He had fetched a screwdriver from the tin under the stairs. Most of the screws in the tin belonged to things they no longer had, but he tipped them onto a folded tea towel and looked through them anyway.

Elsie waited for him to finish before taking the towel.

The plug was sound. He checked the fuse, then screwed the casing together and tried the lamp in the socket by the cooker. It came on at once.

“There,” he said.

“It does sometimes.”

He switched it off and on again. This time it stayed dark.

For a while neither of them touched it. Outside, a blackbird pulled at something in the narrow bed along the path. Elsie watched it through the window while Arthur wound the flex around the base.

“I’ll take it into town,” he said.

“Not today, you won’t. They shut at twelve on Thursdays.”

After tea he carried it back upstairs. He set it on her bedside table with the switch turned towards the bed, as she liked it. The bulb gave a small click when he tried it.

Elsie was folding the blanket at the foot of the bed.

“Did you water those pots?” she asked.

“Yesterday.”

“They’ll want it again.”

He went down for the watering can, leaving the screwdriver on the landing windowsill.

---

**hop 281**

On Friday Elsie found a dark patch beneath the outside tap. She moved the watering can away and came back later to see whether the paving had dried.

“You’ll want to look at that,” she told Arthur.

He was at the kitchen table, opening a letter with the handle of a teaspoon. He laid the spoon across the envelope.

“Which bit?”

“The tap. It’s been doing it a while.”

He went out after lunch. There was a drop hanging from the spout, and while he watched, another gathered behind it. He tightened the handle with both hands.

“You’ll only make it harder for me,” Elsie said.

She was standing in the doorway with the bread board. He stepped aside so she could shake the crumbs over the bed beside the path.

In the afternoon he turned off the supply and took the tap apart. The washer came out flattened along one edge. He carried it into the kitchen on a saucer.

“Don’t leave that there,” Elsie said. “I’ve just wiped it.”

He found the envelope from the morning’s letter and slipped the washer inside. The letter itself concerned a change to their insurance. He read it again, then put it in the drawer with the others.

That evening Elsie tried the little lamp twice before reaching across him to switch on the ceiling light.

“Leave it for now,” he said.

She settled back against her pillow. Downstairs, the loose parts of the tap lay on newspaper beside the back door. Arthur had written WASHER on the envelope, although he knew what was in it.

**hop 280**

On Saturday Arthur bought a washer for the outside tap. He had taken the old one with him in an envelope, but the man in the shop said there were two sizes it might be.

“Depends how much it’s worn.”

Arthur bought both. At home he put the envelope on the kitchen sill, behind a jar containing three paintbrushes. Elsie was in the garden hanging out sheets.

“Was it busy?” she asked through the open window.

“Not particularly.”

“There was something else we wanted.”

He waited, taking the washers out of their little paper bag.

“It’ll come back to me,” she said.

He changed the washer after tea. The nut was stiff, and he fetched a cloth to put round it so the spanner would not mark the metal. When he turned the water on again, the tap gave a short cough. He watched it for a minute. Nothing fell.

Next door, someone was moving something across a floor. Arthur looked towards the fence, then folded the cloth and put it in his pocket.

Elsie had brought the sheets upstairs. On the landing she stood holding a pillowcase against her chest.

“Light bulbs,” she said.

“Which sort?”

“For the little lamp.”

He looked into the bedroom. The lamp was on his side, with its shade tilted towards the wall.

“I can go Monday.”

Before bed, Elsie sorted another load of washing. Arthur emptied his pockets onto the dressing table. He put the damp cloth over the radiator and the spare washer beside his watch. The old washer was still in the envelope downstairs.

---

**hop 279**

On Sunday Elsie took the washing in before it was quite dry. She spread Arthur’s shirts along the backs of the dining chairs and left the basket beside the door.

“There’s a peg missing,” she said.

Arthur was looking for the television supplement. He lifted the newspaper, then the envelopes beneath it.

“One of the wooden ones?”

“The blue one with the spring.”

He found the supplement folded inside a catalogue. There was a programme he had meant to watch, but it had been on the night before.

After lunch he went into the garden. The line hung loose between the apple tree and the hook by the kitchen window. He tightened it a little, though there was nothing on it now.

From next door came the sound of a tap running. It stopped, started again, then stopped. Arthur stood with his hand on the line until a drop of water travelled down to his cuff.

At the far end of the garden, a towel had been left over the fence. It was brown, with a darker band along one edge. He could not remember seeing it before.

“Did you find it?” Elsie asked when he came in.

“No.”

She was matching socks on the table. One grey sock lay apart from the others.

“I’ll have another look tomorrow,” he said.

That evening he put the kitchen bin out. The towel was gone. Upstairs next door, a light came on behind the small window and stayed on while he rinsed the lid under the outside tap. He left it upside down to drain.

---

**hop 278**

On Monday Arthur found a peg in the border and set it on top of the fence. By teatime it had gone. He mentioned this to Elsie while she was measuring rice into a cup.

“It might have fallen.”

“It was well on.”

She tipped the rice into the saucepan and ran water over it.

Next door had been quiet since the van came. There had been two men, neither wearing a coat, and a blanket caught under the legs of the wardrobe. Arthur had watched them free it. Afterwards the younger man returned for a lampshade, carrying it upside down against his chest.

The car was still there most mornings. Sometimes it stood facing the other way.

On Tuesday Elsie bought too many apples. She arranged them in the blue bowl, then put three in the cupboard because the bowl would not hold them.

“You could take some round.”

Arthur turned an apple until he found the little label.

“He may not eat them.”

“Most people eat apples.”

He peeled the label off and stuck it to the edge of his plate.

On Wednesday the wind lifted the felt on the shed roof. Arthur saw it from the bedroom but by the time he went outside it had lain flat again. Near the fence he stopped to pick up an empty compost bag. There was a pale strip of wood showing where the panel had pulled away from its post.

He folded the bag and put it in the shed. The apples remained in the cupboard.

---

**hop 277**

The rain began on Thursday while Arthur was at the post office. He waited under the awning with a woman who had a parcel tucked inside her coat, then walked home with the newspaper over his head.

Elsie had put a towel on the chair beside the radiator.

“Not that one,” she said when he reached for it. “That's for the floor.”

He left his jacket on the back of another chair.

From the kitchen window they could see the loose fence panel moving. It tipped towards their garden, paused, then settled back. Next door's washing line had been taken down, though the pegs were still in a basket on the step.

“He's home,” Elsie said.

“His car's home.”

Arthur folded the wet newspaper and put it beside the sink. The crossword had come through on the back of a furniture advertisement.

On Friday the rain stopped. Elsie went out to gather the fallen branches while Arthur held the green bin open. Most were too long to fit. He broke them across his knee, carefully at first, then with more force when she went back for another armful.

The loose panel stood straight in the still air.

“Perhaps it only needs a nail,” she said.

“Perhaps.”

After lunch he looked in the cupboard under the stairs. There were screws in an old tobacco tin and a hammer with paint on its handle. He moved a pair of shoes to reach farther in, then put them back.

That evening Elsie left the last of the loaf in its paper bag. Neither of them wanted toast.

---

**hop 276**

On Saturday Elsie found a damp patch behind the bread bin. She moved the bin to the other end of the counter and wiped the wall with a cloth, then called Arthur in from the hall.

He touched the patch with the backs of his fingers.

“Could be condensation.”

“It's only there.”

He looked up at the ceiling, although there was nothing to see on it.

They had been meaning to have the kitchen painted since the spring. Elsie had brought home three cards from the hardware shop, each with a row of pale colours. For a while they had stood against the sugar bowl. Now they were in the drawer with the appliance instructions.

Arthur went outside without putting on his jacket. At the corner of the house he stopped and looked at the gutter. Something grew over its edge, a small tuft with reddish stalks.

When he came back, Elsie was cutting the bruised part from an apple.

“I'll do it tomorrow,” he said.

“Will the ladder reach?”

“It reached before.”

She put the good pieces on a saucer and pushed it towards his place.

Later they went to the supermarket. Arthur stayed with the trolley while Elsie returned a packet of biscuits to the shelf. At the checkout they saw the man from next door carrying flowers and a bottle in a cardboard sleeve. He nodded, and Arthur nodded back.

“Could have asked him then,” Elsie said in the car.

“About what?”

“The fence.”

Arthur waited for a woman to finish crossing behind them before he reversed.

---

**hop 275**

On Sunday Arthur took the stepladder out of the shed to clear the gutter above the kitchen. Elsie stood below him with a bucket, though he had said there would be very little to bring down.

There was half a bucket of wet leaves and a tennis ball, grey on one side.

“That won't be ours,” she said.

He pressed it with his thumb. It gave without coming back into shape.

“Next door, perhaps.”

“They haven't had children there for years.”

He dropped it into the bucket. When he came down she pointed to a dark mark on his trouser knee, and he brushed it, making it larger.

After lunch the rain started. Arthur stood at the kitchen window while Elsie wrapped the remains of the meat. From there he could see the whole length of the fence. One panel had begun to lean towards their side, but the concrete posts were sound.

“We ought to say something before the winter,” Elsie said.

“I'll catch him outside.”

“You said that last time.”

Arthur went to put the ladder away. In the shed he had to move two folding chairs before it would lie flat against the wall. Behind the chairs was a tin of green paint, left by the previous owners. He lifted it by the handle and put it down again.

That evening Elsie telephoned her cousin. Arthur watched a programme about a railway journey with the sound turned low. When she came back into the room, he asked whether there was any news.

“Nothing much,” she said.

---

**hop 274**

The letter arrived on Monday with a circular for replacement windows. Arthur opened the circular first, standing by the front door, and put it straight into the wastepaper basket.

His sister had written on both sides of a small sheet. She was coming to the district for an appointment. There was a photograph she remembered, taken in the garden before the fence was changed, and she wondered whether he still had it.

Elsie read the letter after breakfast.

“Which garden?” she asked.

“Ours. At home.”

“She doesn't say who's in it.”

“No.”

He folded the sheet along its original creases. The envelope had torn unevenly, and one corner of the paper caught as he slid it back inside.

On Tuesday Elsie changed the beds. In the spare room she found a cardboard box on the chair and moved it onto the bed while she vacuumed. Arthur came upstairs when the machine stopped.

“I was going to look through those,” he said.

“I haven't touched anything.”

The box contained old calendars, a broken torch and several packets of photographs. He lifted a packet, then set it down again.

“The albums are in the cupboard,” Elsie said.

“I know where they are.”

She wound the flex around the cleaner. He held the door while she carried it onto the landing.

That night he left the letter beside the telephone. In the morning Elsie moved it to wipe the little table, then put it back with the address facing upwards. Neither of them mentioned whether his sister would be staying anywhere nearby.

---

**hop 273**

On Wednesday evening Arthur brought the photograph albums down from the spare room. He put them on the dining table, where Elsie had already laid two mats for supper.

“Not there,” she said. “Something might spill.”

He moved them to the sideboard. The bottom album had left a pale rectangle in the dust upstairs, and he had meant to take a cloth back with him.

“Did you find the one she wanted?”

“I think so.”

Elsie lifted the lid of the saucepan. There was no need to stir it, but she did.

“You could telephone and tell her.”

Arthur opened the top album. The pages made a dry sound as he turned them. In one photograph his sister stood beside a car, holding a handbag in front of her with both hands. Someone had cut away the person standing next to her. A sleeve remained at the edge.

“She might like to look herself,” he said.

Elsie brought the plates over. He closed the album and pulled out her chair, though she was going back for the potatoes.

During supper they discussed the loose catch on the spare-room window. Arthur said it would last until the weather improved. Afterwards he washed up while Elsie dried, putting the cups away first so there would be room for the plates.

“We could ask her tomorrow,” she said.

He passed her a saucer.

“There isn't much to give her.”

“There's the baker's.”

Arthur emptied the bowl. Elsie waited until he had finished running the tap before asking whether his sister still took sugar.

---

**hop 272**

On Thursday Elsie went to the shops before lunch. She had written butter on the back of an envelope, although there was enough in the dish for another day. At the baker’s she asked for the small fruit cake.

“The round one?”

“Yes,” she said. “That will do.”

Arthur was in the dining room when she came back. He had moved the chair from beside the window and was holding a damp cloth.

“There was something on the sill,” he said.

Elsie put the cake on the table, still in its paper bag. A little water had run down the wall beneath the window. She fetched a dry cloth from the kitchen and handed it to him.

“Your sister likes this one.”

Arthur looked at the bag.

“She used to get the other sort. With the icing.”

“That was at Christmas.”

He rubbed the wall, then examined the cloth. Elsie took it from him before he could lay it on the table.

After lunch she telephoned his sister to ask whether four would suit. They spoke about the buses. There was one that stopped near the house, but only on the outward journey; going home meant walking to the main road.

“Arthur can walk down with you,” Elsie said.

When she told him, he was opening the window to let the wall dry.

“Of course I can.”

He put the chair back, leaving room behind it for the curtain. Elsie carried the cake into the kitchen and found the tin on the upper shelf. She washed it and stood it beside the sink to dry.

---

**hop 271**

On Friday the dining-room window would not close properly. Arthur pressed his shoulder against the frame while Elsie turned the handle, but when he stepped back there was still a line of daylight at the bottom.

“It’s the catch,” he said.

Elsie put her hand near the gap. She could feel very little, though the curtain moved when she let it fall.

They were expecting his sister at four. Elsie had brought down the smaller tablecloth and laid it with the fold running across the table. There were places for three, with the milk jug covering a pale circle in the wood that the cloth did not quite reach.

At half past three his sister telephoned. Arthur stood in the hall with his back to the dining room.

“No, another time,” he said. “There’s no point coming out if you’re like that.”

Afterwards he took one cup and saucer back to the cupboard. Elsie left the extra plate where it was.

They had tea at four anyway. Arthur cut the cake into slices, then pushed two of them together under the lid of the tin.

“Will she want you to ring tomorrow?” Elsie asked.

“She said she’d ring us.”

When they had finished, Elsie shook the cloth over the sink. A few crumbs caught in the draining board. She folded it once and put it on the back of a chair.

Arthur was looking through the small tin of screws he kept in the kitchen drawer.

“Nothing in here fits anything,” he said.

Elsie wiped the draining board with her hand.

---

**hop 270**

On Saturday Elsie washed the smaller tablecloth with Arthur’s shirts. There was a mark beside the hem which she rubbed with soap before putting it in the machine. Afterwards she could not find the mark, though she held the wet cloth up to the light.

Arthur had gone out for a newspaper and returned with a packet of screws.

“They hadn’t any of the shorter ones,” he said.

“What are those for?”

“The window.”

He put the packet beside the telephone, where it remained unopened. At eleven he made coffee. Elsie had hung the shirts outside, but the cloth was over the drying rack in the kitchen because she did not want it brushing against the wall.

“You’ll have to move that if you’re using the oven,” he said.

“I know.”

In the afternoon they watched a programme about a house being sold. The couple buying it wanted to knock the dining room into the kitchen. Arthur said something about the wall being load-bearing, but nobody on the programme mentioned it.

Before bed Elsie checked the tablecloth. It was dry along the edges and still damp where it crossed the wooden bars. She turned it over.

“There’s the blue one,” Arthur said from the doorway.

“That’s upstairs.”

“I can fetch it.”

“Not now. You’ll wake yourself up.”

He waited while she switched off the kitchen light. On the stairs she remembered that the blue cloth would need ironing, but by then Arthur was telling her he thought they were nearly out of toothpaste.

---

**hop 269**

On Sunday Arthur took the suitcase down from the wardrobe because Elsie wanted the blue tablecloth. She had put it away after Christmas, though neither of them could remember why it had needed to go in a suitcase.

“Under the summer things,” she said.

There were two bathing towels on top, still folded as they had come home from the coast. Arthur set them on the bed.

“We could use these.”

“There’s nothing wrong with the towels we use.”

He found the tablecloth wrapped around a glass dish. Elsie carried both downstairs, leaving him with the suitcase open.

They were expecting nobody. The smaller cloth was in the wash, and she disliked putting plates straight onto the polished wood.

After lunch Arthur stood at the kitchen window. Rain collected along the edge of the shed roof and fell in a single thick thread beside the door.

“That gutter wants seeing to.”

Elsie rinsed the dish, although they had not used it.

“Put it on your list.”

He had written the list on an envelope in the spring. It was somewhere near the telephone.

Later she went upstairs to close the spare-room window. The rain had stopped, but the air coming in was cold. She pulled the handle down as far as it would go and straightened the net curtain where it had caught beneath the frame.

From downstairs Arthur asked whether she wanted tea.

“In a minute.”

She looked for somewhere to put the bathing towels, then laid them back inside the suitcase. One corner of the lid rested against the television.

---

**hop 268**

On Monday Elsie found a damp patch beneath the spare-room window. She pressed it with her thumb, then went downstairs to ask Arthur to come and look.

He was sorting the post into two piles. Neither pile was for keeping.

“Is it wet?” he asked.

“That’s what I’ve come to tell you.”

Upstairs, he moved a cardboard box away from the wall. The bottom left a dark rectangle on the carpet.

“We ought to have the window open more.”

“It has been open.”

He tried the catch and left it as it was.

The box contained Margaret’s school books, with a pair of curtains folded over them. Elsie lifted the curtains out and held them against herself while Arthur examined the cardboard.

“I’ll find another box,” he said.

“There are some in the shed.”

“Those have things in.”

She put the curtains on the bed. There was barely room beside the suitcase and the small television they had brought up when the new one arrived.

Margaret rang that evening. Elsie told her about a neighbour’s hip operation and asked whether the children had started back at swimming. Arthur sat opposite her, turning the damp box a quarter-turn at a time on an old newspaper.

“Your father’s here,” Elsie said.

Margaret had to go.

On Tuesday they left the bedroom door open. Passing it on his way to dress, Arthur stopped and pushed the box farther from the wall with his slipper.

At breakfast Elsie asked whether the heating had come on.

“Not yet,” he said, and reached behind him to feel the radiator.

---

**hop 267**

On Wednesday the boiler made a noise while Elsie was washing a cup. She turned the tap off and waited. From the sitting room Arthur asked whether someone was at the door.

“It’s this again,” she said.

He came in with his finger between the pages of his book. They stood beside the sink until the noise stopped.

“I’ll ring in the morning.”

“You said that last time.”

“Yes, well.”

She dried the cup and put it in the cupboard. Arthur stayed where he was, looking at the pipes.

The man came on Thursday before they had cleared breakfast. Elsie moved the toast rack to the sideboard, though he did not go into the dining room. He opened a black case on the kitchen floor and asked how long they had had the boiler.

Arthur said twelve years. Elsie said it was put in before Margaret’s wedding.

“Longer, then,” Arthur said.

The man wrote something down.

That afternoon they emptied the cupboard beside it. There were shopping bags inside one another and a tin of white paint with a brush laid across the lid. Arthur took the paint out to the shed. When he came back, Elsie was sitting at the table.

“He’ll need to go upstairs,” she said.

“What for?”

“The pipes.”

Arthur folded the bags into a square. He pressed them flat with both hands, but they rose again when he let go.

“I’d better clear a way.”

“There’s nothing on the stairs.”

He picked up the bags and looked for somewhere to put them.

---

**hop 266**

The man who fitted the boiler left on Friday with the old one strapped to a trolley. Arthur held the front door while Elsie stood by the stairs, holding a screw he had found on the landing.

“Is this yours?” she asked.

The man felt in his pocket before looking at it.

“No, you can throw that away.”

She put it on the hall table.

There was dust upstairs, although the work had mostly been in the kitchen. On Saturday she took a damp cloth into the spare room and wiped the windowsill. Arthur followed her with the vacuum cleaner. He carried it against his chest, the loose hose knocking each stair.

“You should have asked,” he said.

“I haven’t done anything yet.”

They had moved the bed so the man could lift a floorboard. Now there was a pale rectangle on the carpet where it usually stood. Elsie knelt and picked up a hairpin.

“Whose is that?” Arthur asked.

“Mine, I expect.”

She hadn't worn one for years. She put it in her apron pocket and stood with a hand on the mattress.

They pushed the bed back, but it stopped short of the wall. Arthur said the castor was turned the wrong way. He would see to it after tea.

At half past nine she heard him upstairs. A floorboard creaked, then there was a small knock against the wall.

“Have you done it?” she called.

He came to the top of the stairs.

“It’s near enough.”

She waited for him to come down before switching off the hall light.

---

**hop 265**

On Sunday Elsie found a glass beside the spare-room bed. There was a little water in it and something grey settled at the bottom. She carried it down with her finger over the rim.

“Did you put that there?” she asked.

Arthur looked up from the booklet that had come with the new boiler.

“I might have.”

She emptied it into the sink. Outside, a shirt sleeve had caught round the washing line. The rest of the shirt hung still.

After dinner they went upstairs to look for the guarantee. Arthur said it was in the drawer beneath the window, though Elsie remembered putting it with the gas bills. They found a birthday candle, a key neither of them recognised, and an envelope marked Photographs in her handwriting.

“Shall I put these somewhere?” he said.

“They are somewhere.”

He closed the drawer, but it came open again an inch.

The table had been hers before they married. Her father had brought it on the bus, wrapped in an old curtain, and Arthur had met him at the stop. She remembered watching from the window as they changed hands with it.

“It could go downstairs,” Arthur said.

“Where?”

He bent to examine the legs rather than answer. One shifted when he touched it.

“We’ll see tomorrow.”

That evening she put the guarantee on the mantelpiece. It had been with the gas bills. Arthur was asleep in his chair, the boiler booklet open across his chest. She left it there and went up to turn the spare-room light off.

---

**hop 264**

On Monday Arthur brought the small table down from the spare room. He had taken the legs off to get it round the turn in the stairs, and one of the screws was missing.

“It was always like that,” he said.

Elsie spread a newspaper on the kitchen floor. There was dust along the underside where nobody had thought to wipe.

“We bought it for the window,” she said.

“There wasn’t room.”

“There was then.”

He knelt carefully, holding the loose leg against its bracket. She watched him search the newspaper with his palm, although he had already said the screw was missing.

In the spare room the carpet was darker where the table had stood. Elsie moved a box over the mark, then moved it back. From the window she could see Arthur crossing the lawn with the table under one arm. He set it inside the shed and came out rubbing his knee.

She opened the wardrobe. Two empty hangers touched with a small wooden sound. On the shelf was a folded blanket she had washed in April and put away before it was quite dry. She took it downstairs.

“Is someone coming?” Arthur asked.

“It wants airing.”

He held the back door for her while she carried it through. At the line she shook the blanket once, raising a smell of soap and cupboard. Arthur had gone towards the front of the house. She heard the gate strike its post, then strike it again.

“Leave it,” she called.

He came back without answering and took the peg bag from her.

---

**hop 263**

The gate had been catching since the wet weather. Arthur said the post would straighten when the ground dried, but each morning Elsie found it standing open against the lavender.

On Tuesday a woman delivering leaflets came as far as the kitchen window.

“I couldn’t make it shut,” she said.

“Nobody can,” Elsie told her.

Arthur was at the sink. He rinsed his cup again after the woman had gone.

“There’s a man used to do that sort of thing,” he said.

“Then ring him.”

His number was somewhere in the drawer with the guarantees. They emptied it onto the table after tea: instructions for a cooker they no longer had, two lengths of string, a packet of photographs in a chemist’s envelope.

Elsie put the photographs beside her plate.

“Those aren’t guarantees,” Arthur said.

“I know what they are.”

There were six, all taken in the same garden. In one, a young man held a chair above his head, carrying it through a doorway. Someone’s hand had come into the corner of the picture.

“We could write,” she said.

Arthur folded the cooker instructions along their old creases.

“You could.”

On Wednesday Elsie bought writing paper at the post office. The assistant offered her a packet of ten envelopes, though there were already envelopes with the paper.

“I’ve enough, thank you.”

At home she tried three pens on the back of the receipt. One worked if she pressed hard. She left it beside the packet and went outside to collect the washing before the light went.

---

**hop 262**

Elsie wrote the letter on Thursday, while Arthur was finding someone to mend the latch on the gate. She had put a newspaper beneath the writing paper, although the table had been marked for years.

When he came in, she covered the second page with the first.

“Did you get him?”

“His son answered. He doesn’t do gates now.”

“Someone else, then.”

Arthur hung his jacket on the chair. There was a bowl of apples between them, each with its small label still attached.

“Shall I say you’ve been doing the garden?” she asked.

“If you like.”

She added something near the bottom. He stood behind her, not close enough to read it.

“Nothing about my knee.”

“I haven’t.”

The address was in an old pocket diary. Elsie had copied it into the newer ones until the year they stopped buying diaries. She checked the number twice, holding the envelope alongside the narrow page.

“Was there a flat number?” Arthur asked.

“Not when we went.”

He remembered carrying a box up some stairs, but that might have been the other place. He took an apple and worked at the label with his thumbnail.

They posted it together after lunch. Elsie gave him the envelope while she buttoned her coat, then took it back at the pillar box.

On Saturday he repaired the latch himself. It shut if you lifted the gate a little. He called Elsie out to try it.

“That’ll do,” she said.

On Sunday she made enough potatoes for three. The ones left over went into a covered dish in the fridge.

---

**hop 261**

On Monday the envelope came back. Arthur recognised it before he picked it up: the blue paper, the stamp Elsie had pressed down with the back of a teaspoon. Someone had drawn a line through the address without quite covering it.

He left it beside her place at the table.

“Perhaps it needs another stamp,” he said.

Elsie read the small printed label.

“No.”

She put her shopping list over it and went to get her coat. When she came home, the envelope had been moved to the sideboard, where they kept the things that needed attending to.

There were batteries there from the kitchen clock, though neither of them knew whether they were the old ones or the replacements. Elsie put them in a drawer to make room for her bag.

On Tuesday Arthur walked to the post office for a book of stamps. He bought the ordinary sort, not the ones with birds, and asked whether blue envelopes were still all right.

The woman said they were.

At home he told Elsie there had been a queue almost to the door.

“You should have gone after lunch.”

“I was out.”

She was unpicking the hem of a curtain they no longer used. He watched her lay each short length of cotton on her knee.

“Have we got any writing paper?” he asked.

“In the bureau. Under the guarantees.”

He found three sheets and a birthday card with no envelope. The returned letter stayed on the sideboard. Before bed, Elsie lifted it to dust underneath, then set it down with the address facing the wall.

---

**hop 260**

On Wednesday Arthur found the address book behind the telephone directory. He brought it into the kitchen with one finger keeping his place.

“Is this still right?”

Elsie dried her hands before taking it. The entry was in her writing, though she remembered Arthur making the call when they first had the number.

“The postcode changed,” she said.

“When?”

“When she moved.”

He took the book back and looked at the crossed-out line beneath the address.

“That was the flat.”

“Yes.”

Outside, the window cleaner lifted his bucket over the gate. Elsie reached for her purse, but Arthur already had the money ready on the sill. He had included fifty pence extra, as he always did now, and she wondered whether the man had ever noticed.

Afterwards she went through the fruit bowl. Two apples had softened where they touched. She put one in the bin and turned the other so that its good side faced upwards.

Arthur was still standing with the book.

“You could ring,” she said.

“I thought a letter.”

She folded the dishcloth over the tap. There was a loose thread hanging from one corner; she wound it round her finger until the tip went white.

“Well,” she said, and pulled it free.

That evening they watched a programme about a house being built beside a river. Neither of them liked the windows. At ten Arthur checked the back door and Elsie put the milk bottles out, although there was no delivery due.

He noticed them when he drew the curtain, but left them where they were.

---

**hop 259**

On Thursday the post came while Elsie was cutting the bruised part from an apple. Arthur brought it through and put the supermarket leaflet beside her plate.

“Anything else?”

“Something about the insurance.”

He stood by the drawer where they kept the stamps, looking at the leaflet upside down.

“There’s paper in there,” she said. “Under the takeaway menus.”

“I know where it is.”

She cut the apple into quarters and offered him the plate. He took the smallest one.

After lunch Elsie went upstairs to put away the towels. The back room door had caught on the carpet again. She pushed it with her hip, leaving a pale mark on her cardigan where the handle rubbed.

The bed was made, with the narrow quilt tucked beneath the pillows. On the dressing table stood a lamp with no bulb and a dish containing two hair grips. She picked one up. A little rust came off on her thumb.

Arthur called from below.

“Have we only got Christmas stamps?”

“They still work.”

“I didn’t say they didn’t.”

She put the grip back in the dish and opened the window. Across the gardens, someone was bringing in washing, pulling the line hand over hand.

When she came downstairs, Arthur had cleared a place at the kitchen table. A sheet of paper lay in front of him, blank except for the date.

“Tea?” she asked.

“In a minute.”

She filled the kettle for two. While it boiled, she rinsed the knife and left it on the draining board, then moved it into the rack.

---

**hop 258**

On Friday Elsie washed the bedding from the back room, although nobody was expected. She carried it downstairs against her chest, the pillowcase slipping twice from the bundle.

Arthur was at the kitchen table with the gas bill and a pencil.

“Are we having someone?” he asked.

“No. It needed doing.”

He moved the bill so she could put the sheets down. Beneath it was an envelope addressed in his own handwriting. She saw their daughter’s name, then the sheets covered it.

The washing machine took a long time to fill. While they waited, Arthur turned the cold tap on and off, listening.

“It wants a washer.”

“Can you still get those?”

“Of course you can.”

She measured powder into the little drawer. Some spilled along the edge, and she brushed it in with her finger.

By lunchtime the rain had stopped. They carried the bedding outside between them, keeping it clear of the path. Arthur held the basket while Elsie pegged a sheet to the line. There were only three wooden pegs left; the plastic ones opened differently, and he passed them to her with their jaws already apart.

“Leave the basket,” she said. “I’ll fetch it.”

But he took it back to the kitchen.

Before bed he checked the tap once more. He tightened it with the corner of a tea towel until the drops stopped falling.

Elsie stood in the doorway, holding the dry pillowcase she had just ironed.

“You’ll not get it open tomorrow.”

“I’ll go to the shop,” he said.

She folded the pillowcase again, though it was already folded.

---

**hop 257**

On Saturday Arthur went out for a washer and came back with a packet of batteries. The shop had changed hands. Things that used to be in drawers were hanging on cards behind the counter, and the young man had asked him what size.

“I said ordinary,” he told Elsie.

She was cutting the bad part from an apple. She turned it in her hand and made another cut.

“You should have taken the old one.”

“It’s still on the tap.”

He put the batteries beside the bread bin. There were others in the kitchen drawer, loose among the string and takeaway menus, but he couldn’t remember which had come from the radio.

Elsie divided the apple between two saucers. They ate standing up.

Later she went upstairs to find a pillowcase. The back room door caught on the carpet, and she pushed it with her shoulder. Someone had folded the small blanket across the foot of the bed. She could not remember doing it herself.

From below came the sound of Arthur opening the drawer, then shutting it too hard.

“Did you find them?” she called.

“What?”

“Nothing.”

She opened the window a little. The room smelled of warm cloth, though the radiator was cold. Across the gardens, a man was drawing a blue cover over a stack of timber, bending to tuck each corner underneath.

That evening the kitchen tap began dripping again. Arthur put the washing-up sponge beneath it.

“That’ll do for tonight,” he said.

Elsie stood at the sink and watched the sponge darken. Then she pulled the curtain across the window.

---

**hop 256**

On Sunday Elsie stripped the little bed in the back room, though nobody had slept in it since August. She carried the sheets downstairs against her chest. At the turn she stopped to move a slipper from the step with her foot.

Arthur was at the kitchen table, sorting batteries into two rows.

“Are those any good?” she asked.

“Some of them.”

She put the washing by the door. Outside, the line hung slack between the house and the concrete post. It had rained during the night, and water remained in the split along the post’s top.

“I’ll wait till later.”

Arthur fitted two batteries into the torch and pointed it under the table. A pale circle appeared on his trouser leg.

“There.”

After tea she took the clean sheets upstairs. There was room in the airing cupboard if she moved the towels, but she left them on the landing chair instead. Above the cupboard a brown mark spread a little way from the corner, thin at its edges.

“Arthur.”

He came to the bottom of the stairs.

“Was that there before?”

He looked where she pointed.

“Hard to tell from here.”

“You could get the ladder.”

“Tomorrow. It’s behind everything.”

She went into the back room and shut the window. On the sill stood a glass with three pencils in it and a button at the bottom. She took the glass to the bathroom, washed it, and returned the pencils. The button she put in her cardigan pocket.

Downstairs, Arthur had switched on the television. She sat beside him and began folding the towels.

---

**hop 255**

On Monday Arthur brought the stepladder into the hall and left it folded against the wall. Elsie had asked him to look at the stain above the airing cupboard, but by the time he found the ladder she was making lunch.

“After, then,” he said.

She put two bowls on the table. There was enough soup for a third, which she poured into a jug and covered with a saucer.

“Someone could have that tomorrow.”

Arthur broke his bread over his bowl. A crumb caught in the fold of his sleeve, and he brushed it onto the floor.

After lunch he climbed three steps and pressed the ceiling with his thumb.

“Dry.”

“It wasn’t always there.”

“No.”

He came down and carried the ladder out again. Elsie watched from the kitchen doorway, holding the saucer she had decided was too small for the jug.

Later they drove to the shops. Arthur stayed in the car while she bought milk and a packet of biscuits. She also bought a single pear, then put it at the bottom of the bag before returning.

“Much in there?” he asked.

“Not particularly.”

At home she unpacked while he counted the change she had left on the table. The pear had bruised against the milk bottle. She turned that side towards the wall.

That night, taking off her earrings, Elsie noticed a small tear in the sheet beside her pillow.

“I can mend that,” she said.

Arthur looked over from his side of the bed.

“Where?”

She showed him, but he had taken off his glasses.

---

**hop 254**

On Tuesday Elsie washed the sheets, though there were not enough to make a proper load. She added two pillowcases from the drawer and a towel that Arthur said he had only used once.

“It’s in now,” she said.

He stood beside the machine with his hands in his pockets, watching the water rise.

The post came while she was wiping the kitchen windowsill. There was a gas bill and an envelope addressed to the previous owners, who had been gone nineteen years. Arthur wrote NOT KNOWN across it, pressing so hard that the pen went through.

“You’ll need another envelope.”

“They’ll manage.”

He left it by the bread bin.

After lunch Elsie took the washing outside. The grass beneath the line was wet enough to mark her slippers. Arthur was against the shed, looking up into the pear tree.

“There’s still a few,” he said.

“You said they were finished.”

“Most of them.”

She shook out a sheet. For a moment it hung between them, and when she had pegged it he had moved away.

By teatime the washing was not dry. Elsie felt the hem of a pillowcase between her finger and thumb, then left everything where it was.

That evening Arthur searched the drawer under the telephone for a stamp. He found a key neither of them could place and put it on the mantelpiece.

“Leave that there,” Elsie said. “In case we remember.”

Before bed she checked the forecast in yesterday’s paper. Arthur had already turned off the kitchen light. She folded the paper in the dark and followed him upstairs.

---

**hop 253**

On Wednesday morning Arthur brought in a pear from the tree against the shed. He put it beside Elsie's plate, where it left a damp mark on the tablecloth.

“That'll want eating.”

She turned it over. There was a soft brown patch near the stalk.

“Not all of it.”

“No.”

After breakfast she cut away the bad part and divided what was left between two saucers. Arthur had gone to look for his secateurs. His portion stood beside the butter until lunchtime, when she ate it herself.

There were sheets to bring in. Although the air was still, the corners had wound themselves round the line. Elsie unfastened the pegs one at a time and dropped them into the pocket of her apron. From the other side of the hedge came the sound of Arthur opening and closing the shed door.

“Have you looked on the shelf?” she called.

“Which shelf?”

“The one where they go.”

He said nothing more, and presently she heard him cutting.

Upstairs, she put the clean sheets in the airing cupboard. The narrow bed in the back room had a cover over it. A cardboard box of old curtains occupied the chair. She moved the box to reach the window, which had begun to stick, and left it on the landing.

At supper Arthur asked what she had been doing upstairs.

“Putting things away.”

He scraped a little mustard from the edge of his plate.

Later, when the news began, she remembered the box. She went as far as the foot of the stairs before the telephone rang.

---

**hop 252**

On Thursday Elsie took the smaller roasting tin down from the cupboard, then put it back. The larger one was behind it, with the string bag of cutters inside. She lifted these out and set them on the draining board.

“How many are we having?” Arthur asked.

“Three.”

He stood in the doorway with his gardening gloves still on. One finger had worn through, and the nail showed black at the end.

“Only I could do the potatoes.”

“Not with those hands.”

He looked at them, then went out again.

Their daughter had telephoned after the news the night before. Elsie had answered, keeping the kitchen door shut because Arthur liked to hear the weather. There had been a little discussion about trains. Nothing had been said about staying.

When Elsie returned to the sitting room, he asked whether she was well.

“She says she is.”

“And Sunday?”

“About one.”

He nodded and moved his slipper away from the fire. On the television a man was pointing to rain that would reach them by morning.

Now Elsie washed the roasting tin, though it was clean, and dried it with the cloth reserved for glasses. In the drawer beneath the telephone she found last year's card. Arthur had kept it under the book of local numbers. She opened it far enough to see the writing, then closed it and returned it to the same place.

Outside, he was gathering the cuttings into a pile. Every few minutes he stopped to pull his glove back over the exposed finger.

---

**hop 251**

The card had arrived on Friday, although their daughter was coming on Sunday. Arthur slit the envelope with the knife he kept in the hall drawer and read it standing beneath the stairs.

“She's put a stamp on it,” he said.

“Well, it came through the post.”

“I mean she could have brought it.”

Elsie took the envelope from him before he could put that on the mantelpiece too. Inside the card their daughter had written Dad, then something about a lovely day, then her name. There was room beneath it where another name used to go.

On Sunday morning he came downstairs early and fetched the milk. Elsie heard the bottles touch the step, then the door being closed carefully. When she came down he had laid the table for breakfast, using the good marmalade.

“That's nearly finished,” she said.

He turned the jar towards himself, as though the label might tell him otherwise.

After breakfast she sent him to buy carrots. They had carrots, but not enough if their daughter wanted some to take home. He returned with a bag of six and a newspaper he already had.

“They've changed the picture on the front,” he said.

She put the second copy under the sink.

By one o'clock the kitchen windows had misted at the edges. Elsie lifted the meat out to rest and searched the drawer for the carving knife. Behind her, Arthur opened the oven.

“Don't let the heat out.”

“I was only looking.”

“Well, look at something else.”

He closed it gently. She found the knife beneath the rolling pin.

---

**hop 250**

Elsie took three plates from the cupboard and set them beside the breadboard. The fourth came with them, stuck to the one above it by a drop of water. She separated it with her thumbnail and put it back.

“Shall I do the potatoes?” Arthur asked.

“They're done.”

He stood in the kitchen doorway wearing the jumper she had meant to wash. There was a pale mark down the front, possibly toothpaste. She wet the corner of a cloth and held it out to him.

“Where?” he said.

She touched her own chest.

He rubbed at the wrong side, then found it.

Their daughter had telephoned on Thursday to ask what he wanted. He had said nothing, as usual, and she had said that wasn't helpful. Afterwards he had wondered aloud whether nothing sounded ungrateful.

“She knows what you mean,” Elsie had said.

Now he folded the cloth and left it on the radiator.

“Did she say half past?”

“About half past.”

He looked at the clock. Elsie turned down the ring beneath the saucepan, though the water had barely begun to move.

There had been some discussion about inviting her for the whole weekend. The spare bed was made, but neither of them had mentioned it when she called. Elsie had put fresh soap in the bathroom anyway.

Arthur went into the front room. From the kitchen she heard him move the little table away from the window, then move it back.

“She's here,” he said.

“Let her get out of the car first.”

---

**hop 249**

Their daughter arrived with a parcel under one arm and a cake box balanced on her palm. Arthur opened the door before she could ring.

“I saw you parking,” he said.

“It took me three goes.”

“That car opposite doesn't help.”

He took the cake, leaving her to manage the parcel and her coat. Elsie came from the kitchen, drying her hands on a towel she then carried back with her.

They ate at the small table. Their daughter asked whether the boiler had been fixed, and Arthur said it had, although there was still a noise when the heating came on. Elsie asked about her journey. Neither asked about the empty chair at her daughter's own kitchen table.

Afterwards Arthur opened the parcel, carefully peeling the tape away from the paper.

“You can tear it, Dad.”

“It's good paper.”

The cardigan was blue, with large brown buttons. He held it against himself before putting it on. Their daughter reached across and straightened the seam at his shoulder.

“There,” she said.

Elsie brought in the cake. She had found two candles in the drawer with the birthday cards and the spare batteries.

“One for each of us,” Arthur said.

“It's not my birthday.”

“You can help.”

Their daughter took out her phone, then put it down again while Elsie searched for matches. In the end Arthur supplied his lighter, though he had stopped smoking years before.

He blew both candles out at once. Elsie waited for the wicks to cool, then lifted them by their clean ends and laid them on the cake box.

---

**hop 248**

The candles had left two small holes in the icing. Elsie cut around them, giving their daughter the piece with the sugar flower.

“That used to be your favourite part.”

Their daughter smiled and moved the flower to the edge of her plate.

Arthur was still wearing the cardigan she had given him. The room had grown warm, but taking it off seemed ungrateful. He put a finger inside the collar and pulled it away from his neck.

“Is it comfortable?” she asked.

“Very. Just right.”

“I kept the receipt.”

“No need for that.”

Elsie gathered the little forks onto one plate. There were six in the drawer, though she could not remember the last time they had needed all six. One had a bent prong. She had given herself that one without thinking.

For a while they talked about the house next door, which had been sold to people neither of them had met. Arthur said there would probably be building work. Their daughter said perhaps they would only paint it.

“You never know,” Elsie said.

The clock on the mantelpiece struck nine. Their daughter looked towards it, then at her wrist, where she no longer wore a watch.

“I ought to make a move.”

“Already?” Arthur said.

“She has work in the morning.” Elsie began stacking the plates, then stopped. “Have you time for another cup?”

“Better not, Mum.”

Arthur stood to help her with her chair, but she had pushed it back herself. He remained standing, holding his napkin. After a moment he folded it and put it beside his plate.

---

**hop 247**

Their daughter put on her coat in the hall, then came back for the book she had brought to return. It was still in its carrier bag beneath her chair.

“You can keep it a bit longer,” Elsie said.

“I’ve finished it. That’s why I brought it.”

“Oh, yes.”

Arthur took the bag from her and laid it on the sideboard. He had lent her the book at Christmas, but could no longer remember whether he had liked it.

“Good journey back,” he said.

“I’m only going across town, Dad.”

He nodded. There had been roadworks, though, somewhere along her route. He had meant to find the notice in the paper.

Elsie went into the kitchen for the cake. She had wrapped a generous piece in foil and pressed the edges together with her thumbnail. When she returned, their daughter was looking at her telephone.

“Here. For tomorrow.”

“You shouldn’t have cut so much.”

“It needs eating.”

Their daughter slipped the parcel into her handbag. She kissed Elsie, then Arthur, reaching up to him although he had already bent his head.

“Lovely evening,” she said. “And happy birthday again.”

At the gate she turned and raised one hand. Arthur raised his too, but she was already walking away.

Elsie stayed beside him until they heard the car start. Then she shut the door, carefully lifting the handle before turning the key.

“She’s left her scarf.”

A green scarf hung from the banister. Arthur touched its fringe.

“She’ll want that.”

“Not tonight,” Elsie said. “It’s mild tonight.”

---

**hop 246**

By half past nine the house was quiet again. Elsie folded the paper napkins that hadn’t been used and put them in the drawer with the candles. There were three candles left in the packet, although the box showed six.

“Did she take some cake?” Arthur asked.

“I wrapped it for her.”

“That’s not what I asked.”

“It’s gone, Arthur.”

He looked at the table, as though their daughter might have left it under something. Only the marks of the plates remained on the cloth, and a small heap of crumbs by his chair.

“She said she’d ring on Sunday.”
