Martin put the invoice on the spike. At closing time he walked home instead of waiting for the bus. Rain had collected in the toes of a pair of shoes outside the cobbler’s, where somebody had left them beneath the window.

Elsie was mending a pillowcase at the table. She had spread newspaper under her work because the cloth was still damp from tea.

“He wants me Saturday evening.”

“How late?”

“Six.”

She drew the needle through and held the seam up to the light.

“That’s something, then.”

He did not tell her what Mr Bell had said. He went upstairs and took the coins from his trouser pocket, laying them on the chest of drawers without letting them touch. There was enough for the bus the next morning and a little more.

On Thursday Elsie bought half a pound of sausages. One split in the pan, and she gave herself that one. Martin noticed but said nothing.

After supper he found her looking in the drawer beneath the sideboard.

“Have we any stamps?” she asked.

“Not unless there’s one in the rent book.”

She checked between its pages, then closed it and pushed the drawer shut with her knee.

---

**hop 135**

On Friday Elsie put a letter beside his plate. It had been opened neatly with the kitchen knife.

“Your sister,” she said.

Martin took off his coat before reading it. There was a photograph inside, showing the new baby on a blanket. On the back his sister had written the weight and the date.

“They want us on Sunday.”

“I read it.”

He propped the photograph against the sauce bottle. The baby’s eyes were shut, and one hand lay open beside its head.

“We could catch the ten past eleven.”

Elsie brought the potatoes over in the saucepan.

“And come back on what?”

“There’ll be a train back.”

“I mean the fare.”

He moved his plate to make room for the saucepan. They ate without turning on the wireless. Afterwards he put the letter in the sideboard drawer where they kept the rent book and old guarantees.

In the morning she asked him to bring a loaf when he finished. She was still in her dressing gown, standing at the sink with the cold tap running over a cloth.

At the shop Mr Bell had left a list of jobs under the till. Martin swept the entrance, unpacked six enamel basins and changed the price on a coil of clothesline. Shortly after dinner a man brought in a broken cupboard catch. Martin found one that would fit, but the man said he would have to ask his wife about the finish.

Later Martin opened the screw drawer. Someone had emptied a returned order into it, folding the paper bag small and leaving that there too.

---

**hop 134**

At closing time on Saturday Mr Bell came down from the office carrying the wage packets. He laid them along the counter, clear of the tray where Martin had been counting brass screws.

“Quiet again,” he said.

Martin tipped the screws into their drawer. There were three sizes mixed together, and he had spent most of the last hour separating them.

“People mend things themselves now,” Mr Bell said.

“They still need the screws.”

Mr Bell smiled, as if Martin had meant to be amusing, and went to fetch his hat.

Outside, Martin opened his packet with a thumbnail. The notes were folded round the slip in the usual way. He counted them before putting them into his wallet, then pushed the empty packet into a litter bin.

Elsie had asked him to bring a loaf. At the baker’s he waited behind a woman choosing cakes for a box. Each time the assistant reached for the string, the woman remembered another person who might be coming. Martin looked at the clock above the bread shelves.

When he got home, Elsie was taking washing off the rack in front of the fire.

“Did you get paid?” she asked.

He put the loaf on the table and counted out the housekeeping money. She left it where it was until she had folded a pillowcase.

“There’s enough bread for tomorrow,” he said.

“I can see that.”

That evening he stayed in his work shoes. Before bed, he found the good pair under the wardrobe, with dried mud still caught along the stitching.

---

**hop 133**

On Sunday Martin took his good shoes to the kitchen and spread a newspaper beneath them. The polish had hardened round the edge of the tin. He worked the brush into the middle, where there was still enough to use.

Elsie was sorting the bills at the table. She put the gas bill aside and held up an envelope addressed in blue ink.

“That’s your sister’s.”

“I know.”

“She’ll be expecting an answer.”

He set one shoe on the newspaper and picked up the other. His sister had paid for their mother’s grave to be cleared and had sent him the account. There was no letter, only a line on the back saying that half would do.

“I’ll send it tomorrow,” he said.

Elsie laid the envelope beside his plate.

After dinner they went as far as the recreation ground. A man was putting away the bowls in the pavilion, fitting each into its place in a wooden box. Martin waited at the gate while Elsie shook a stone from her shoe. The benches were wet, though it had not rained since breakfast.

On the way home they passed Mr Bell outside the tobacconist’s. He had a small parcel under his arm and lifted two fingers from it when he saw them.

“He doesn’t look worried,” Elsie said when they had gone past.

“What would he be worried about?”

“You’re always saying trade’s poor.”

Martin had no answer that seemed worth making. At home he put his sister’s envelope in his jacket pocket, then checked that he had enough money in his wallet.

---

**hop 132**

On Monday the rain came through above the office window. Martin moved the ledger before the drops reached it and put a saucer on the sill. There was a brown mark on the ceiling from the last time.

“You’ll want something deeper,” Mr Bell said.

Martin fetched a pudding basin from the cupboard where they kept the tea things. All morning the water fell into it with a sound that became less distinct as the basin filled.

At lunchtime he ate his sandwiches without unfolding the paper completely. Elsie had put in mustard, although he had told her the week before that it brought on his indigestion. He ate them all the same. Afterwards he walked to the post office for a postal order and stood behind a woman returning a pension book.

The clerk asked whether she had the proper form.

“I was told to bring this,” the woman said.

Martin looked at the rates for parcels until it was his turn.

That evening Elsie asked if anything had happened at work.

“The roof’s leaking again.”

“Did you tell him?”

“He saw it.”

She folded the cloth they used under the teapot and laid it across the chair back to dry.

On Tuesday morning the basin was half full. Martin carried it to the lavatory, holding it away from his trousers. A little water spilled on the passage floor. He wiped it with the cloth from the tea cupboard, then rinsed the cloth and hung it over a pipe.

By eleven the rain had stopped. He returned the ledger to its place.

---

**hop 131**

On Tuesday afternoon Mr Bell asked Martin to stay a moment. He stood beside the filing cabinet with his hand on the top drawer, as though he had come for something and forgotten what it was.

“There’ll be someone starting tomorrow. Perhaps you could show him the system.”

Martin said he could.

“Nothing complicated. Just how we do things.”

After Mr Bell had gone, Martin finished adding the column in front of him. The total was three pounds short. He went through it again and found he had carried a seven instead of a ten.

Miss Carter’s desk had been empty for nearly a fortnight. Someone had removed the calendar, leaving a pale rectangle on the blotter. In the middle drawer Martin found two paper clips, a peppermint covered in fluff, and a ruler with the first inch broken off. He put the paper clips in the tray on his own desk. The ruler he left.

At five he emptied her wastepaper basket, although there was only a strip of adding-machine paper in it.

His bus was waiting when he reached the stop. He could have run, but the conductor had already turned towards the platform bell.

Elsie was peeling potatoes when he got home.

“You’re late.”

“Missed one.”

She moved the peelings aside with the back of the knife. There was a letter beside his plate, addressed to the previous tenant. They had lived in the house eleven years.

“Another,” she said.

Martin put it on the mantelpiece. After supper he polished his shoes and set them on newspaper by the kitchen door.

---

**hop 130**

The new boy had been given the desk nearest the radiator. Martin showed him where the duplicate invoices were kept and which drawer needed lifting before it would close.

“You get used to it,” he said.

The boy tried the drawer twice, then left it open.

For most of Wednesday Martin checked figures which had already been checked. At half past ten he took his cup to the little room behind the stairs. There were six cups on the draining board, although only five people now used the office. The sixth had belonged to Miss Carter. Nobody had taken it when she left.

He rinsed his spoon and put it beside the others.

At home there was a catalogue on his chair. Elsie had turned down a page showing garden benches.

“We could have one against the back wall,” she said.

“The ground’s not level there.”

“You could see to that.”

He looked at the bench. It was photographed on a lawn without washing lines or a coal bunker. Underneath, in smaller print, it said that assembly was required.

“I’ll have a look,” he said.

On Thursday the boy asked whether he could bring in a photograph of his wife.

“No reason why not.”

“Only I noticed you hadn’t any.”

Martin opened the ledger at the next clean page. He had once kept a photograph in his wallet, but the transparent pocket had split and the corners caught whenever he took out a note.

At lunchtime he bought a packet of envelopes. There were envelopes at home, but he couldn't remember where.

---

**hop 129**

On Friday they asked Martin whether he would rather have the presentation before lunch or at four, when the drivers were back.

“Whichever suits,” he said.

Mr Phelps stood beside his desk with a sheet of paper folded lengthways. Someone had written Martin’s name at the top.

“Four, then. We don’t want anyone missing out.”

Martin moved a delivery note from one tray to the other. It had been put in the wrong place by the new boy, who was twenty-three and had worked in two other offices already.

“I’ll leave the books straight,” Martin said.

“There’s no worry about that.”

At home Elsie asked if wives were expected.

“He didn’t say.”

“Well, you could ask.”

He hung his jacket on the back of the chair, then took it upstairs. When he came down she was peeling potatoes into a bowl lined with newspaper.

“I don’t suppose it’s that sort of thing,” he said.

On Saturday he had his hair cut. The barber asked whether he wanted anything for the weekend, as he always did, and Martin said no, thank you. Afterwards he walked past the ironmonger on his way to the bus. There was something he had meant to get, but a woman was struggling out through the doorway with a clothes airer and he stepped aside for her.

He remembered on the bus.

That evening Elsie moved the sitting-room chairs to sweep underneath. Martin helped her put them back, fitting each foot into its mark in the carpet.

“They might be better by the window,” she said.

He straightened the rug between them.

---

**hop 128**

On Sunday Martin went out to the shed to look for a washer. The kitchen tap had been dripping since Thursday, though Elsie said it stopped if you turned it a little further.

“That’s how they get worn,” he had told her.

The washers were in a tobacco tin with some screws and the small key to a cupboard they no longer had. He tipped everything onto a newspaper. There were three washers, none the size he wanted.

Elsie came to the back door.

“Your tea’s made.”

“I’ll be in.”

He put the screws back first, then the washers. He held the key for a moment before dropping it into the tin.

In the kitchen Elsie had laid two biscuits on his saucer. A catalogue was open beside her cup.

“We could go on the bus one morning,” she said. “Have a look at these chairs properly.”

“There’s nothing wrong with those.”

“I didn’t say there was.”

He sat down without taking off his jacket. The tap gave a drop, then another. He could buy a washer on his way home tomorrow, provided the ironmonger was still open.

“Not this week,” he said.

“No. I meant after.”

He picked up a biscuit. Elsie folded the corner of the catalogue page and closed it.

Later he checked his briefcase for the order-book key. It was in the inside pocket where he always kept it. He put the briefcase by the front door, then went upstairs to choose a shirt. There were five clean ones hanging in the wardrobe.

---

**hop 127**

On Monday they asked Martin whether he would prefer the presentation before lunch or at the end of the day. Mrs Bell stood beside his desk with the staff diary open.

“Whichever suits,” he said.

“Friday afternoon, then. About four.”

He moved his hand so she could rest the diary on the desk. There were already two appointments in the space for Friday. She wrote his name beneath them and drew a line under it.

The new man was due on Tuesday. Martin had cleared the other desk, putting the old invoices into a box that no one had yet agreed to take downstairs. He found a ruler behind the telephone and tested its edge against his palm. One corner had broken off. It would still do for most things.

At home he told Elsie about Friday.

“Will there be something to eat?” she asked.

“I expect so.”

“Then I won’t get anything much in.”

On Tuesday the new man arrived carrying a coat over his arm, although it had been raining. Martin showed him where to hang it. Afterwards they sat together at the cleared desk and went through the order book.

“Is all this on the computer?” the new man asked.

“Some of it.”

Martin turned back three pages to show him a correction. The new man leaned closer, following the figures with the cap of his pen.

On Wednesday Martin brought a shopping bag folded inside his briefcase. He left it there until the others had gone to lunch. Then he opened the bottom drawer.

---

**hop 126**

By Thursday Martin had emptied the bottom drawer. There were two bus timetables, a tin of drawing pins and a scarf Elsie had asked about the winter before. He folded the scarf into his shopping bag. The drawing pins belonged to the office, though he remembered buying them himself.

The new man came over after lunch with a question about the heating.

“It goes off at half past four,” Martin said. “Unless you turn the dial in the cupboard.”

“Which cupboard?”

He showed him. There was a piece of cardboard wedged beneath the door to stop it catching on the carpet. Martin bent to put it back when they had finished.

“You won’t remember all of it at once,” he said.

The new man had brought a notebook, but he did not write that down.

At five Martin took his mug to the small sink. He washed it and dried it with a paper towel, going round the handle with his thumb. Then he put it back on the shelf. There was still tomorrow.

At home Elsie was peeling potatoes over a sheet of newspaper.

“Did you bring your things?” she asked.

“Most of them.”

She looked into the bag and lifted out the scarf.

“I thought that had gone.”

“So did I.”

After supper he checked his wallet for the ticket he would need in the morning. His weekly pass ran until Sunday. He took it out, looked at the date and slipped it behind the photograph of Elsie that had come from her old driving licence.

---

**hop 125**

On Friday they gave Martin a clock, though there was one above the door and another on the wall behind his desk. The new man held the box while he took off the paper. Someone had found scissors for the ribbon.

“You’ll have to come back and see us,” the new man said.

Martin put the scissors beside the telephone. “These belong downstairs.”

There was a card as well. He read the names while the others took pieces of cake from a plate. Some had written only their names, and some had added a line about fishing or putting his feet up. He had never owned a fishing rod.

At home Elsie cleared a space on the mantelpiece. The clock needed a battery neither of them had.

“I’ll get one tomorrow,” he said.

On Saturday he went into town after breakfast. He bought the battery and a packet of washers, then stood outside the ironmonger’s with the small paper bag in his hand. Usually there were other things to get, things someone at work had asked him to pick up. He checked his pockets before crossing to the bus stop.

Elsie was at the kitchen table when he returned, looking at a coach company’s leaflet.

“They do three nights at the coast,” she said. “Weekdays are cheaper.”

He put the washers in the drawer beneath the cutlery.

“We could look,” he said.

She left the leaflet beside his chair. Later, when she asked whether he wanted it kept, he said yes, he hadn’t finished with it.

---

**hop 124**

On Sunday Martin brought the small case down from the top of the wardrobe. Elsie was changing the bed. She waited with the clean sheet gathered against her while he set the case on the mattress and tried the catches.

“Are you going somewhere?”

“No. Thought I’d see if this was any use.”

One catch opened. The other needed pressing with both thumbs. Inside were a hotel sewing kit and a folded carrier bag. He laid them on the bedside table.

“It’s smaller than I remembered.”

“It always was that size.”

He brushed a white mark on the lining. Elsie pulled the sheet over her corner and waited again.

At dinner he told her the new man had asked where they kept the spare keys. There had always been two sets, but nobody could find the second.

“Did you look in that tin?” she said.

“What tin?”

“The one you mentioned. When the cupboard got flooded.”

“That was years ago.”

Afterwards he went into the garden, though there was nothing he particularly wanted to do there. He moved an empty pot from the step to the wall. From the kitchen Elsie watched him test the back gate, lifting it slightly before closing the bolt.

The case remained on the bed until evening. She put the sewing kit inside it, then the carrier bag.

“Shall I leave this down?”

“Better had.”

She stood it beside the wardrobe, with the handle outwards. Before getting into bed, Martin moved it farther back so he could open the bottom drawer.

---

**hop 123**

On Monday Elsie washed his shirts as usual. There were four in the basket and one hanging behind the bedroom door, with a bus ticket in the pocket. She put the ticket on his chest of drawers beside the loose change.

“Will you need all these ironing?” she asked.

Martin was looking for his slippers.

“Might as well.”

She filled the iron from the jug she kept under the sink. Outside, a neighbour was trimming the hedge, stopping every few minutes to speak to someone passing. Martin stood at the window until she asked him to move; his shadow was across the board.

On Tuesday he came home earlier than she expected. She had the kitchen drawer tipped out on newspaper and was testing the old batteries in the little radio.

“Something wrong with it?”

“Only seeing which ones are worth keeping.”

He picked up a battery and turned it between his fingers.

“You can get a tester for these.”

“I’ve nearly finished now.”

He put it down among the ones she had already tried. Neither of them could remember which pile it belonged to.

At tea he said there might be a meal on Friday. Someone was arranging it, but they hadn’t settled where.

“Will wives go?”

“I don’t think it’s that sort of thing.”

She passed him the potatoes.

Later, while he watched the news, she ironed the shirts and hung them with their collars facing the same way. The blue one had begun to fray at the cuffs. She put it at the far end of the rail.

---

**hop 122**

On Wednesday Martin brought home a small plant in a plastic pot. He set it on the draining board, where Elsie found it when she came down to start the tea.

“Is somebody leaving?” she asked.

“It was on the windowsill.”

She touched the soil. It had drawn away from the sides of the pot.

“Does nobody water things there?”

“I used to.”

He took off his jacket and hung it over the back of his chair. There was a pale mark on one sleeve, and she rubbed at it with her thumb before fetching a cloth.

“They’re moving the desks round,” he said. “Putting that new section by the window.”

“Already?”

“They need the space.”

She held his sleeve flat against the table and dabbed the mark. He watched for a moment, then went out to the shed.

They had spoken about it in July, when the first meeting was called. At the time there had been forms to complete and figures to compare. Martin had borrowed a calculator from the office, although there was one in the kitchen drawer. He said the buttons on theirs were too small.

Now the figures were written on a sheet beside the telephone. Elsie had stopped putting shopping lists on the back of it.

After tea she watered the plant and found a saucer for it. Martin came in carrying a tin of screws he said needed sorting.

“Where shall we put this?” she asked.

“Anywhere it gets some light.”

She moved the fruit bowl along to make room. He sat down with the tin between his knees.

---

**hop 121**

The letter had arrived on Thursday, folded so that the signature showed first. Martin read it standing beside the hall table, with his coat still buttoned. Afterwards he put it back in the envelope and laid his keys on top.

“Is that it, then?” Elsie asked.

“They’ve put it in writing.”

She waited, but he bent to loosen his shoes.

For thirty-eight years there had been somewhere he needed to be by half past eight. Even on holiday he woke early, washed, and sat dressed while she decided what they might do. Now the firm had sent three paragraphs and a telephone number for anyone with questions.

On Sunday afternoon he cleared out the bag he took to work. There were old payslips, two screwdrivers, and a packet of biscuits held shut with an elastic band. Elsie watched him test a pen on the corner of a newspaper. It worked, but he put it with the things to be thrown away.

“You could leave that till tomorrow,” she said.

“I’m doing it now.”

At supper he told her there would probably be a presentation. Someone had been collecting money; he had seen the envelope pass behind a filing cabinet.

“You’ll have to look surprised.”

He moved a piece of potato through the gravy.

Before bed she made the sandwiches while he checked the front door. She nearly asked whether they would be giving him lunch, but he had gone upstairs. She cut the bread as she always did, one straight line down the middle, and listened to him moving about above her.

---

**hop 120**

On his last morning, Elsie woke before the alarm and lay listening to a van reverse somewhere beyond the gardens. Martin was on his side, facing the wardrobe. In the light between the curtains she could see the pale line where his hair had been cut too short above the collar.

She got up without switching on the lamp. Downstairs, the kitchen smelled faintly of the cabbage they had eaten the evening before. She opened the back door while the kettle boiled, then closed it because of the cold.

His sandwiches were already in the refrigerator. She had wrapped them in foil rather than putting them in the plastic box, in case there was no convenient way of bringing the box home. Now she wondered whether he would notice.

When he came down he was carrying a tie.

“This one?” he said.

“If you like.”

He draped it over the back of his chair and went to shut the door she had already shut. His hand rested on the handle for a moment.

“There’s no hurry,” she said.

“I know what time it is.”

She put the teapot on the table. They had been given it by her sister, who always asked after it when she visited. A chip beneath the spout was turned towards Martin.

Later, Elsie would remember the tie as blue, although the one he wore in the photograph was brown. She would remember other things incorrectly too. But she was certain about the letter, and about how little either of them had said.

---

**hop 119**

The letter had arrived in February, when the path was still green along the edges. Martin put it beside his plate and ate his toast before opening it. Elsie knew what it was from the envelope. For several weeks there had been envelopes like that, with windows showing his name.

“Well?” she said.

“It’s all as expected.”

He folded the sheet along its original creases. She waited for him to say something about the money, but he asked whether there was any marmalade left.

Afterwards she found the letter in the drawer with the appliance guarantees. She read it standing up, one hand holding the drawer open. There was a number to telephone if anything was unclear.

In March they talked about the back bedroom. Martin said he could strip the paper himself once he had the time. Elsie said the paper was perfectly good. Neither of them had liked it when they chose it.

As the date approached, he began bringing things home in small quantities: a ruler, two packets of envelopes, a pair of shoes she had forgotten he owned. He placed the shoes beneath the stairs with newspaper still pushed into their toes.

“Will you want sandwiches on Friday?” she asked on Wednesday night.

“I should think so.”

“Only I wondered if they’d be taking you somewhere.”

“Nobody’s said.”

She bought his usual loaf. On Thursday she ironed five shirts, then hung four at the far end of the wardrobe. The fifth she left on the chair, with his trousers folded underneath.

---

**hop 118**

On his last morning Martin left at the usual time, although they had told him there was no need to come in before ten. Elsie watched him stop at the gate and check his pockets. For forty-three years there had been something in each one he might need.

He came home carrying a cardboard box and a bottle of wine neither of them liked. The box held his mug, a photograph of Elsie taken before her sister’s wedding, and a stapler he said was his.

“Did they make a fuss?”

“Enough.”

She had bought a piece of haddock for tea. While she coated it in flour, he stood behind her reading the instructions on the wine.

For the first week he got up with the alarm. He fetched the milk, wiped the condensation from the bedroom window and asked what they were doing that day. Elsie found herself mentioning appointments she would ordinarily have kept to herself.

On Thursday he went back for the umbrella he had forgotten. He was away nearly three hours and returned without it.

“Someone’s put it somewhere.”

By the second Monday he had cleared a space in the shed. There were screws in an old biscuit tin, hinges wrapped in newspaper and three handles from a cupboard they no longer owned. He brought one into the kitchen to show her.

“Solid brass. You don’t throw that away.”

She moved the sugar bowl so he could set it down.

That evening he asked whether anything had come from the pension people. She said these things took time.

---

**hop 117**

The letter arrived on Tuesday with a leaflet offering cheaper windows. Elsie put the leaflet in the recycling and left the envelope beside Martin’s place, where he would see it without her having to say anything.

He came downstairs wearing the good trousers he had begun wearing every day.

“Anything?” she asked.

“Only the pension people.”

He opened it with the bread knife. There were three sheets, one of them blank on the back. He read the first twice, then folded all three along their original creases.

“Well?”

“They’ve got the dates wrong.”

“Will that matter?”

“It’ll matter to somebody.”

After breakfast he took the letter upstairs. Elsie heard the wardrobe door slide open, then catch on the bit of carpet he had promised to trim. When he came down again, he had changed into his old trousers.

On Wednesday she went to the library and renewed a book she had not started. Outside the chemist’s she met Jean, whose husband had found two mornings a week driving for the hospital.

“It gets him out,” Jean said.

Elsie said Martin had plenty to keep him occupied. Walking home, she remembered the gutter overflowing onto the back step, and wished she had named something else, though Jean had not asked.

By Friday the letter was back beside the telephone. Someone had written a number on the envelope, pressing hard enough to score the paper underneath.

At half past five Elsie peeled potatoes into a sheet of newspaper. Through the open window came the small, irregular sounds of metal being moved from one container to another.

---

**hop 116**

On Friday evening, Elsie found Martin in the shed, sorting screws into the shallow drawers he had brought home when the works closed. A length of guttering lay across the bench. He had been meaning to fix it above the back door since February.

“Your tea’s getting cold,” she said.

“I’m coming.”

She stayed in the doorway while he tested a screw against a hole in a bracket. Beyond him, the little window had gone dark enough to show her reflection.

“Did you ring them?”

“They said they’d write.”

“You told me that last week.”

He put the screw into a drawer and closed it with two fingers.

“There’s a list, apparently.”

In the kitchen she had left the grill on low. The fish had dried at the edges, though neither of them mentioned it. Martin asked whether there were any potatoes left, and she passed him the saucepan instead of getting up to serve them.

Afterwards they watched a programme about a couple buying a house abroad. Elsie thought the stairs in every house were unsuitable. Martin said people didn’t think about stairs until they had to.

Before bed, she checked the calendar beside the telephone. There was nothing written for the following week except the day the bins changed.

“Do you need anything from the shops tomorrow?” he asked.

“Something for the fruit bowl.”

He looked at the two apples, wrinkled around their stalks.

“I’ll see what they’ve got.”

She turned the calendar back against the wall. Upstairs, he ran the hot tap for a long time before she heard him begin to wash.

---

**hop 115**

The post came late on Saturday, after Martin had gone to buy the paper. Elsie separated the envelopes on the hall table, putting the circular for replacement windows straight into the recycling. There was one for him and a catalogue addressed to the woman who had lived there before them, twenty-three years ago.

She carried both into the kitchen.

When he returned, he set a bag of pears beside the fruit bowl. They were hard, but the man had said they would soften.

“There’s something for you,” she said.

He opened the envelope with his thumb, tearing a strip from the sheet inside. Elsie watched him fit the strip back against the edge before reading.

“Is that the appointment?”

“Yes. They’ve given me a cancellation.”

“That’s good, then.”

He read it once more. She put the pears in the bowl, moving the older fruit to the top.

“Will you want me to come?”

“I don’t know how long it takes.”

“I could bring a book.”

He folded the letter and slipped it into the newspaper.

On Sunday they went to the garden centre for compost. Martin pushed the trolley while Elsie chose a packet of wallflower seeds. At the café she asked whether he wanted anything, and he said a coffee would do. She bought a slice of fruit cake as well, with two forks.

They sat near the glass doors. Each time someone went outside, a draught lifted the corner of the paper napkin beneath his cup. Eventually he moved the cup onto it properly, and they finished the cake.

---

**hop 114**

On Monday evening, Elsie found the letter beneath the newspaper. Martin had folded it along a new crease, leaving the hospital’s name on the outside. She smoothed it against the table with the flat of her hand.

“Do you need to take this with you?”

“I expect so.”

“I’ll put it by the telephone, then.”

He was watching a programme about a house neither of them liked. The young couple wanted to knock two rooms into one. Martin said they would regret it when the heating bill came, and Elsie stayed standing until the picture changed.

“Thursday,” she said.

“Yes.”

There was a cup beside his chair with a little tea still in it. She took it through to the kitchen and washed it with the supper things. Outside, a sleeve of his blue shirt lifted on the line, then dropped. The washing could stay there until morning. It would only need airing.

When she came back, Martin had turned off the television.

“Going up?” he asked.

“In a minute.”

She checked the back door and moved the letter away from a damp ring beside the telephone. Upstairs, water ran into the basin. She waited for it to stop before turning out the hall light.

In bed, he read while she rubbed cream into her hands. Neither mentioned the letter again. She set the alarm for seven, though there was nothing particular to get up for, and lay facing the window. After a while he switched off his lamp. She heard him arrange his slippers on the floor before settling beside her.

---

**hop 113**

Elsie woke before the alarm and reached across to stop it ringing. Martin was already sitting on the edge of the bed, easing his feet into his slippers. He had put them neatly together the night before, but one had worked its way under the bed.

“Leave it,” she said. “I’ll get it.”

“I’ve got it now.”

She watched him pull the heel up with one finger. Then she turned back towards the window, where the curtains had parted just enough to show a pale square of sky.

They had not decided who would drive. There was no need to decide yet; the appointment wasn’t until Thursday, and today was only Tuesday. Still, she had checked the bus timetable while the potatoes boiled yesterday. The changes would make it awkward coming home.

Martin stood and took his dressing gown from the hook.

“Milkman’s been,” he said.

“Has he?”

“Heard him a while ago.”

After he had gone downstairs, Elsie folded back the bedclothes on both sides. His book lay face down beside the lamp. She marked his place with an old shopping list and closed it, then opened the window to the first notch.

In the garden, the washing line had come loose from its cleat. She would ask him to tighten it before breakfast, or do it herself while he shaved. It only needed winding round once more.

She put on her cardigan and went to fetch the washing, remembering as she reached the door that she had not brought the basket up.

---

**hop 112**

The towels had been left on the landing overnight. Elsie gathered them against her chest and stood for a moment outside the spare room, looking at the strip of sunlight beneath the door. She had meant to open the curtains in there yesterday.

Downstairs, Martin was putting the milk away. She heard the bottles touch on the doorstep, then the bolt slide home.

“Any post?” she called.

“Not yet.”

There wouldn’t be, of course. It was barely eight.

She carried the towels into their bedroom and laid them on the bed while she sorted the things on the chair. His trousers could go another day. There was a receipt in one pocket, folded lengthways, for petrol bought on Monday. She put it beside his watch on the dressing table.

The envelope from the surgery lay there too. Martin had brought it upstairs last night, although everything they needed was written on the separate sheet in the kitchen. She moved the watch a little so it wasn’t resting on the envelope.

“Shall I do toast?” he called.

“I’m coming down.”

She picked up his blue shirt with the towels. One cuff had turned inside out, and she pulled it straight before adding the bundle to the basket. At the bottom of the stairs she stopped to free a towel caught beneath the handle.

Martin had taken two mugs from the cupboard. He stood holding them until she passed, then set them beside the kettle.

“Quite bright out,” he said.

“Yes. I’ll get these on the line.”

---

**hop 111**

Elsie found a tissue in the pocket of Martin’s blue shirt before she put it in the machine. She held it up to him where he stood in the kitchen doorway.

“Caught it this time.”

He nodded, as though she had told him something about the weather.

The shirt was hardly worn. He had put it on for their visit to the surgery, then hung it over the bedroom chair when they came home. She could have returned it to the wardrobe, but there were already towels to wash and the forecast was good until lunchtime.

“Did you want this for anything?” she asked.

“No.”

She pushed it into the drum and shut the round door with her knee. Martin stayed where he was while she measured the powder. On the windowsill, the small clock had stopped again. Neither of them mentioned it; the oven clock was right.

“I’ll get the prescriptions today,” she said.

“They said after eleven.”

“I know. There’s other things I can do first.”

He moved aside to let her pass, though there was room enough. At the cupboard she reached for the breakfast plates and found one still damp underneath. She wiped it with the corner of the tea towel.

Martin filled the kettle. He put in more water than they needed, then tipped some back into the sink. While it boiled, Elsie set out the butter and marmalade and cleared yesterday’s newspaper from his place.

“Two slices?” she asked.

“One’ll do.”

She put three in the toaster and turned the dial down a little.

---

**hop 110**

The washing machine finished while they were having breakfast. Neither of them moved until it gave its second set of beeps.

“I’ll do it,” Martin said.

Elsie watched him push his chair back. He had left the crust of his toast, although there was still marmalade on his plate. Once she would have pointed this out. Now she took both plates to the sink and ran the tap until the water warmed.

By the kettle lay the slip from the surgery. She had written their names on it in separate columns, with the tablets beneath. Martin’s list was longer, but hers had the difficult one, the name she could never say without pausing halfway through.

“Anything else while I’m out?” she asked.

He was bending over the machine, gathering clothes against his chest rather than using the basket beside him.

“We need pegs.”

“There are pegs in the drawer.”

“Those wooden ones.”

“They work.”

He carried the washing through to the garden. A sleeve trailed behind him, leaving a dark, uneven line on the kitchen tiles.

Elsie dried her hands and put the slip in her purse. In the hall she changed out of her slippers, using the long shoehorn Martin had bought after his operation. He had never liked it himself.

Through the open back door she heard him shake out a sheet. She waited for him to call something, then put on her coat.

“I won’t be long,” she said, not loudly enough, and pulled the front door closed, checking with her thumb that the latch had caught.

---

**hop 109**

At the chemist’s, Elsie had been asked whether she still wanted both prescriptions put in the same bag.

“Yes,” she said. “We’re still at the same address.”

The girl behind the counter smiled without looking up. She was new, or perhaps she had only changed her hair. Elsie waited while she stapled the folded papers to the bag.

On the way home she stopped outside the hardware shop. There were spring bulbs in shallow wooden crates, each variety shown on a card with a photograph too bright to be useful. Martin had said they ought to put something along the fence. He said it most years, usually when the ground was too hard or too wet.

She bought a packet of yellow tulips and carried it separately from the medicines.

Their gate had begun to drag again. She lifted it by the latch, feeling the familiar pull across her shoulder, and noticed a white peg lying in the border. One of its halves was missing. She picked it up anyway.

From the garden came the squeak of the rotary line turning. She could see Martin’s head above the fence, then his hand reaching for the last of the washing.

“I’ve got your tablets,” she called.

He raised his hand again, though she could not tell whether he had heard.

She went in through the front door and set the chemist’s bag beside the telephone. The tulips she put underneath it, then moved them to the windowsill, where they would be seen. A little soil had escaped from a hole in the packet. She brushed it into her palm.

---

**hop 108**

Martin had brought the washing in before it was quite dry. Elsie found him folding a pillowcase at the kitchen counter, matching the corners with a care he never gave to his own shirts.

“It could have had another half hour,” she said.

“Looked like rain.”

Outside, the paving was still pale. A blackbird stood beside the empty clothes basket and pulled at something between the slabs.

She took the pillowcase from him and laid it over the back of a chair.

“Did you do the small room?”

“Which one?”

“Ruth’s.”

“No. Were we doing that?”

“Only the sheets. They get stale.”

Martin put two socks together, then separated them again. One had a blue line across the toe.

“Nobody’s slept in there.”

“I know that.”

He opened the drawer where they kept the tea towels. It caught on the handle of a wooden spoon, and he pushed it shut before trying again.

Elsie went upstairs. The room smelled faintly of furniture polish and the lavender bags her sister gave her every Christmas. She opened the window an inch. On the desk, beneath a glass paperweight, was a timetable from the year Ruth had learned to drive. Elsie lifted the paperweight, wiped its round base with her thumb, and put it back.

She left the sheets for another day.

Downstairs, the post lay under the letterbox. There were two offers addressed to the previous owner, dead now for eleven years, and a white envelope with their own name showing through its window.

“Anything interesting?” Martin asked.

“Not unless you want a conservatory.”

“We’ve nowhere to put one.”

---

**hop 107**

The telephone rang while Elsie was sorting the envelopes. She had made two piles on the hall table, though everything in both would eventually go into the recycling.

“Could you get that?” Martin called.

She was already reaching for it.

At first there was only a soft rushing sound. Elsie pressed the receiver closer and looked through the glass in the front door. Someone had left the gate open again.

“Mum?”

“Yes.”

“It’s me.”

“I know.”

From the kitchen came the clatter of the cutlery basket being emptied. Elsie moved an envelope away from the edge of the table.

“Are you busy?” Ruth asked.

“No. Just the usual.”

“I can ring back.”

“What is it?”

Ruth took a breath that sounded as though she had turned away from the telephone to do it.

“Is my room still there?”

Elsie looked at the stairs. On the third step was a cardigan she had been meaning to take up since yesterday.

“Of course it is.”

“I wondered if I could come.”

“When?”

“Today, if that’s all right.”

“Have you had anything to eat?”

There was a small pause.

“I’ll get something at the station.”

“Don’t get one of those sandwiches. There’s bread here.”

“All right.”

Elsie drew the address book towards her and opened it, although she knew where the station was and Ruth hadn't given her a time.

“Shall your father meet you?”

“I’ll take a taxi.”

“He wouldn’t mind.”

“I know. I’d rather.”

“Bring a coat, then. It’s turned cold.”

“I will,” Ruth said. “Bye, Mum.”

---

**hop 106**

Elsie put the telephone back on its stand and stayed beside it, looking at the address book underneath. It was open at a page she hadn't needed.

Martin came in from the kitchen with a teaspoon in his hand.

“Was that Ruth?”

“She’s coming.”

“For the day?”

“She asked if the room was still there.”

He looked towards the stairs, as though some alteration might have taken place without his knowing.

“What did you say?”

“What do you think I said?”

He rubbed the bowl of the spoon with his thumb. There was something dried on it that the dishwasher hadn't shifted.

“I'll get milk,” he said.

“We've got milk.”

“She used to have that other sort.”

“That was years ago.”

Elsie closed the address book. Ruth's old number was still in it, crossed out once in pencil, then again in ink. She had meant to buy a new book, but there were too many addresses she would have to decide whether to copy.

“Did she say how long?” Martin asked.

“No.”

Outside, the council lorry stopped by the gate. They listened to the bottles falling, then to the engine starting again.

“The bed will need doing,” Elsie said.

“I changed it after your sister.”

“That was in April.”

She went upstairs and opened the airing cupboard. The good sheets were under a stack of towels. She pulled one out carefully, holding the towels in place with her chin.

Martin followed her onto the landing.

“Open the window a minute,” she said. “It'll be stuffy in there.”

---

**hop 105**

The spare-room window had swollen shut. Martin pressed the heel of his hand against the frame while Elsie stood behind him holding a clean sheet.

“Leave it,” she said. “We’ll only let the heat out.”

“You asked me to air it.”

“That was before.”

He tried once more, then turned the catch back. On the sill, a dead fly lay among the dry leaves of the geranium. Elsie gathered them into her palm and carried them to the wastepaper basket, which still had a paper liner Ruth had made from a Sunday supplement.

They spread the sheet without speaking. Martin’s corner came free when he pulled the opposite one tight. Elsie waited for him to notice.

“What time did she say?” he asked.

“After lunch. If the trains are running.”

“They’re running.”

“She has to get to the station first.”

He tucked his corner in again. There was a pale rectangle on the wall where the bookcase had stood. They had moved it downstairs for the television, and neither of them had mentioned moving it back.

Elsie shook out the duvet. Its cover smelled faintly of the cedar blocks she put in the drawers, though she could never find them when she wanted to replace them.

“Will that be warm enough?” Martin asked.

“There’s the blanket.”

“The blue one?”

She nodded. He went to fetch it from their room. Elsie smoothed the pillowcase with both hands, then slid her fingers between the mattress and the wall to check that nothing had fallen there.

The room was colder than she had thought.

---

**hop 104**

Elsie found the hot-water bottle at the back of the airing cupboard, folded beneath a pillowcase. The rubber had stuck to itself. She pulled the sides apart and held it to the landing light, looking for cracks.

“Wouldn’t trust that,” Martin said from the stairs.

“It was perfectly good last winter.”

“Last winter we didn’t use it.”

She put it back. He was carrying the small suitcase they kept Christmas decorations in, which had been standing beside the spare-room wardrobe since January. There was nowhere else for it except their own room.

“Under our bed,” she said.

“I know.”

Downstairs, the kettle had boiled without switching itself off. Elsie lifted it from its base and waited for the noise to stop. Through the kitchen window she could see the line of snow overhanging the roof, thickened at the edge like a badly cut slice of cake. Water dripped from one corner onto the step.

Martin came down and filled his mug from the kettle.

“Road looks clear enough.”

“Here it does.”

He stood beside her, leaving room between them for the drawer to open. She took out the bread knife, then could not remember what she had meant to cut.

“Did you tell her about the bathroom door?” he asked.

“No.”

“I’ll have a look before she gets here.”

It needed lifting as you turned the handle. Ruth would remember, unless she expected it to have been repaired.

Elsie laid the knife on the breadboard. Martin drank his tea standing up, watching the drip outside grow quicker.

---

**hop 103**

By eleven, the snow had slipped from the kitchen roof in a single piece, taking the gutter bracket with it. Martin went out to look and came back carrying the bracket in his glove.

“Nothing to screw it into now,” he said.

Elsie moved the breadboard so he could put it down.

Ruth’s train was due at twelve forty. There had been no message since the one from the junction, where she had bought tea and found somewhere to sit. Elsie had read it twice, then set the phone on the windowsill with its screen facing the room.

“She’ll need collecting,” Martin said.

“She said she’d get a taxi.”

“Not today she won’t.”

He took his keys from the hook, although it was too early to leave. His boots stood on newspaper by the back door. A little water had reached the advertisement for fitted kitchens and made the photograph of a cupboard swell.

Elsie wrapped the bread and put it away. She had bought the sort Ruth liked when she was young, with seeds that collected in the bottom of the bag. There was no reason to suppose she still liked it.

“Is the room all right?” Martin asked.

“Yes.”

“Radiator’s on?”

“It’s on.”

He nodded, turning the keys over in his hand.

Upstairs, a door clicked shut in the draught. They both looked towards the ceiling. Elsie had left the spare-room window open for ten minutes after breakfast, then forgotten it until her hands began to feel cold at the sink. The bed, at least, was ready.

---

**hop 102**

The night before, Elsie found a hot-water bottle in the airing cupboard. Its cover had gone stiff along the seam, and when she unscrewed the stopper a few brown flakes fell into her palm.

“Is there another one?” she called.

Martin was in the bathroom. He turned off the tap before answering.

“There used to be.”

She waited, but he did not say where.

In the spare room, the clean sheets lay folded on the chair. Elsie had brought them up that afternoon and left them there while she decided whether to use the heavier blanket. Ruth had always complained of being cold in that room, though she used to sleep with the window open.

Martin came to the doorway, rubbing his hands on a towel.

“Forecast says it'll stop by morning.”

“Then it'll freeze.”

He looked at the bare mattress.

“Shall I do that side?”

They spread the bottom sheet between them. Martin pulled his corner too far down, and Elsie gave him some of hers without speaking. Afterwards he stood holding the pillow while she worked its case over it.

“Did she say how long?” he asked.

“Not exactly.”

He laid the pillow at the head of the bed. Elsie straightened it once he had gone.

Downstairs, she put the bottle beside the kitchen bin rather than inside it. She would test it in the morning. Through the glass in the back door, the garden path was still visible, a dark strip ending at the shed. By the time she switched off the light, its edges had disappeared.

---

**hop 101**

Elsie had taken the good cups down before breakfast. There were six, though only three would be needed, and she washed them all because the shelf had left a grey line around each rim.

Martin sat at the table opening the post with a knife. He put an envelope addressed to Ruth beside the sugar bowl.

“That still comes here,” he said.

“She'll have to write to them.”

“It's only the building society.”

Elsie dried the cup she was holding and turned it over to examine the base. The small crack was no longer small. She put that one at the back.

Outside, snow had filled the channel Martin had cleared at seven. His boots stood on newspaper by the door, the paper dark beneath the heels.

“Will you fetch her?” she asked.

“She said she'd get a taxi.”

“There may not be any.”

He folded the empty envelope and pushed it inside the other papers.

Elsie went upstairs to open the spare room curtains. The window had misted at the bottom, and she wiped it with the corner of a towel. From there she could see the road as far as the bend, where a van was reversing towards the main street.

She moved the little table nearer the bed. Then she moved it back, leaving enough room for a suitcase.

When she came down, Martin was standing at the kitchen drawer with Ruth's letter in his hand.

“I thought she gave a number,” he said.

“On the back.”

He turned it over, although Elsie knew he had already looked.

---

**hop 100**

At eleven Martin went out to clear the path again. Elsie heard the shovel scrape the step, then nothing for several minutes. When she looked through the glass beside the door, he was standing by the gate with one hand in his pocket.

She returned to the kitchen and moved the potatoes to a smaller saucepan. There had been no need to cook so many. Ruth's letter said the train arrived at one, which meant she would either eat before she left or buy something on the way.

Martin came in carrying the shovel.

“Not in here,” Elsie said.

“The shed's locked.”

“Put it in the porch, then.”

He leaned it against the outside wall and shut the door carefully.

They ate at twelve. Martin cut the fat from his meat and left it along the edge of his plate. Elsie had meant to ask him to change the towel in the upstairs bathroom, but when he finished she went and did it herself.

The spare room door was open. On the bed lay a folded blanket and the hot-water bottle, empty, with its stopper beside it. She put the bottle in the cupboard. It was too early for that.

Downstairs, Martin had stacked the plates with the cutlery between them.

“You'll scratch those,” she said.

He lifted the top plate and took out the knives.

Beyond the window a car slowed, its tyres making a soft tearing sound in the snow. Martin reached for his cardigan, then left it on the chair. The car went on.

---

**hop 99**

Ruth arrived after lunch, when the plates were stacked but not yet washed. She stood on the mat while Martin worked at the buckle of her suitcase.

“It's only caught,” she said.

“I can see that.”

Elsie brought a cloth for the water gathering beneath Ruth's boots. Nobody had asked her to take them off. She did so anyway, steadying herself against the wall.

“Have you eaten?” Elsie asked.

“On the train.”

“What did you have?”

“A sandwich.”

Martin freed the strap and carried the case upstairs. One wheel knocked against each tread. Ruth watched until he turned the corner, then put her gloves inside her boots.

Elsie made fresh tea. She used the small pot, although there were three of them, and set another cup beside the milk jug. Through the window the garden seemed shorter than it had in summer. Snow filled the spaces between the flower beds.

“Your father's been looking for the key to the shed,” she said.

“Has he?”

“He says you had it last.”

Ruth pulled out a chair but didn't sit down.

“That was years ago.”

“I know when it was.”

Martin came back without the suitcase. He had taken off his cardigan upstairs and the sleeves of his shirt were rolled unevenly.

“Road's clear as far as the corner,” he said. “They've put grit down.”

Ruth sat and accepted her tea. For a while they discussed the trains, which stations had been closed and whether she would need collecting on Friday. Then she asked if she might use the telephone.

“You know where it is,” Elsie said.

---

**hop 98**

By three the snow along the front path had begun to soften. Martin went out with the shovel and came back carrying a biscuit tin he had found behind the coal bunker.

“That's where it went,” Elsie said.

The lid had rusted at one corner. He put it on a newspaper beside the sink.

“Shall I open it?”

“Not there.”

He moved it to the draining board, but she was already taking the cups through. Ruth had left hers almost full. There was a pale mark where her mouth had touched the rim.

Upstairs, a door closed. Martin looked towards the ceiling.

“She said she wanted to make a call,” Elsie said.

“I didn't ask.”

“No.”

Elsie tipped the tea away and rinsed the cup. From the dining room came the small click of the electric fire being switched off, then on again. Martin was trying the second bar. It hadn't worked since November.

“Leave it alone,” she called. “You'll only have to mend it.”

She took the turkey from the pantry and loosened the foil. Underneath, the jelly had set against the edge of the dish. She scraped some away with a spoon and looked for a plate large enough.

The telephone was on the landing. She could hear Ruth lifting the receiver, though not what she said into it. A few minutes later there were footsteps overhead, crossing from the landing to the room at the back.

Martin returned to the kitchen and washed his hands. Elsie gave him the carving knife.

“Thinly,” she said. “There's still tomorrow.”

---

**hop 97**

Ruth came downstairs after four, carrying the towels from her room.

“They weren’t for washing yet,” Elsie said.

“I wasn’t sure.”

She left them on the bottom stair. In the kitchen, Martin was cutting slices from the turkey, holding the bone with a folded piece of kitchen paper. There was more meat than Elsie had expected. She fetched another plate.

“Will you have some cold tomorrow?” she asked Ruth.

“Perhaps.”

“There’s no perhaps about it. I only want to know what to keep.”

Martin laid his knife across the carving board.

“It’ll keep either way.”

Elsie put the plate back in the cupboard, then took it out again. Ruth stood beside the window, rubbing a clear patch in the condensation with her sleeve. Someone had pulled a sledge across the lawn that morning. The tracks stopped at the apple tree.

“Did you get hold of Janet?” Martin asked.

“She was out.”

“On Boxing Day?” Elsie said.

“Her sister said she’d ring.”

They had their tea at the dining table because the kitchen table was covered with serving dishes. Elsie warmed the gravy and poured it into the chipped jug, turning the handle towards Martin. Ruth took a potato and broke it with her fork.

“You can have more than that.”

“I know.”

For a time there was only the sound of their knives. A car went slowly past the house, its tyres making a wet noise on the road.

Afterwards, Martin carried the carving board into the kitchen. Elsie followed with the jug.

“We ought to do something with that tin,” she said.

---

**hop 96**

By six on Boxing Day, the snow along the garden wall had sunk into narrow strips. Martin stood at the back door in his slippers, tipping the roasting tin so that the fat ran into an old jar.

“Not down the drain,” Elsie said, though he had already found the jar himself.

Ruth was clearing the table. She stacked the small plates on the large ones, with the knives between them.

“Leave those,” Elsie said. “We might have something later.”

“I can wash them again.”

The telephone rang in the hall. Ruth put the plates down and went to answer it. Elsie took the knives from between the plates. One had scratched a line through the gravy.

Martin came in and shut the door carefully behind him.

“Freezing again.”

Elsie held out the tin for him to put beside the sink.

From the hall they heard Ruth say that either would be all right. Then she laughed, quietly, and the receiver knocked against something.

They ate no supper. Martin watched a programme about a railway that had closed, while Elsie sorted the cards on the mantelpiece into piles that would not stand. Ruth sat with them for a while, still wearing her shoes.

At ten she asked where the small suitcase was.

“On top of the wardrobe,” Elsie said.

“I thought so.”

“You’ll need your father to reach it.”

But Ruth brought it down herself. They heard the scrape overhead, and then the small, separate clicks of the catches.

Elsie gathered the cards and put them back along the mantelpiece, with the family ones at the front.

---

**hop 95**

On the morning after Boxing Day, Elsie found a glove beneath the chair in the spare room. She laid it on the bed, beside the cardigan Ruth had not worn all week. The wardrobe door was open. Three empty hangers rested against one another at the end of the rail.

Downstairs, Martin was looking for the road atlas.

“It’ll be in the car,” Elsie said.

“I brought it in.”

“Then under the papers.”

He lifted the newspaper without disturbing the things on top of it. Elsie went through to the kitchen and filled the kettle. There was enough water already, but she tipped it out and began again.

Ruth came down carrying two cups from her room.

“I forgot these.”

“Put them there.”

One had a little tea left in it. Elsie emptied both into the sink.

“What time did you say?” she asked.

“About two. Perhaps earlier, if he gets away.”

Martin appeared in the doorway with the atlas. He had opened it at a page showing the coast.

“There’s a better road now,” Ruth said.

“I know. I was only looking.”

She took an apple from the bowl and turned it in her hand. A soft patch had formed around the stalk. She put it back with the patch underneath.

Elsie asked whether sandwiches would be useful for the journey.

“We’ll stop somewhere.”

“Well, there’s plenty.”

Ruth nodded and went upstairs. They heard a drawer close, then footsteps crossing the ceiling. Martin left the atlas on the dresser, open at the same page. Elsie put the potatoes in a saucepan and carried the peelings out to the bin.

---

**hop 94**

Ruth had brought the little suitcase down before lunch and stood it beside the coats. Elsie moved it away from the radiator. There was a label on the handle from a journey none of them had mentioned.

At the table, Martin asked whether the roads would be busy.

“Not going our way,” Ruth said.

He nodded as though she had given him directions.

They ate the cold turkey with hot potatoes. Elsie had opened another jar of pickles, forgetting the one already on the table. Nobody wanted pudding, but when she cut it into slices they each took some.

Afterwards Ruth washed while Elsie dried. The water was too hot at first, and Ruth stood with her hands above it, waiting.

“You could leave those,” Elsie said.

“There aren’t many.”

Martin came in with the glasses. He put them beside the sink one at a time, then went out again.

Ruth dried her hands on the towel hanging from the oven door. She looked round the kitchen before taking off her apron.

“I think that’s everything.”

Elsie folded the apron along its old creases.

At the front door there was some difficulty with the suitcase handle. Martin offered to carry it, but Ruth had already lifted it clear of the step. She kissed him, then Elsie, and said she would ring when they arrived.

They watched her cross to the car. Someone inside reached over to open the passenger door.

Elsie shut the front door carefully. The catch had been sticking, and it sometimes needed a second push.

---

**hop 93**

On the last afternoon of December, Elsie found a pair of gloves behind the telephone. She carried them into the kitchen, where Martin was folding the extra chairs.

“Are these Ruth’s?”

“Could be.”

“She had some on when she left.”

“Then they won’t be.”

Elsie laid them on the dresser. One finger had been mended with thread a shade lighter than the wool.

Outside, the car was still visible at the end of the road. Its indicator flashed while the driver waited for a gap. From where Elsie stood she could see only the roof and the upper half of the back window. Then a van passed, and when it had gone the car was gone too.

Martin took the chairs into the garage. They had borrowed two from next door, but those could wait until morning.

There was enough food for supper without cooking. Elsie covered the ham and put the potatoes in a smaller bowl. The large bowl would not fit in the refrigerator with everything else.

“Tea?” she asked when Martin came back.

“In a minute. I’ll get the cases down.”

“There aren’t any.”

He stood by the kitchen door, holding the garage key.

“No,” he said. “Of course.”

Later Elsie went upstairs to check that the window was shut. The bedcover had been pulled straight, though one corner was caught beneath a pillow. On the chair lay a folded towel. She touched it to see whether it was damp, then left it where it was.

Downstairs, Martin was looking for something to watch. She closed the door halfway and went to join him.

---

**hop 92**

Through January the spare room remained as it had been left, except for the bed, which Elsie stripped on the first Monday after everyone had gone. She washed the sheets separately from their own and put them away at the bottom of the airing cupboard.

There was a glass on the bedside table with a little water in it. She took it downstairs one morning when she was going for the post.

“Was that up there?” Martin asked.

“Yes.”

“I wondered where it had got to.”

They had five others exactly like it. Elsie put it beside the sink and opened an envelope addressed to both of them. It was the renewal for the house insurance.

On Saturdays Martin went round the rooms with the vacuum cleaner. He did not move the furniture, but he used the narrow attachment along the skirting boards. In the spare room he found a button, brown and slightly larger than the ones on his coat. He left it on the landing windowsill.

Elsie moved it to the sewing tin later that day.

The weather stayed wet. When she opened the spare-room window, the curtain lifted towards her and touched her wrist. Along its lower edge was a grey line she had not noticed before. She rubbed a piece of the fabric between her finger and thumb.

That evening she asked Martin whether there was anything of his waiting to be washed.

“Only what’s in the basket.”

“All right.”

He looked up from his plate.

“Why?”

“I’m making room for a few things.”

---

**hop 91**

At the beginning of February Elsie took the curtains down in the spare room. Martin helped with the hooks he could reach without standing on anything. The others she did herself, using the small steps they kept beside the boiler.

“Shall I leave these here?” he asked.

She had already filled a saucer for them.

The room looked larger with the window bare. On the sill was a pale rectangle where a photograph had stood before Christmas. Elsie wiped around it, then went over the whole sill with the damp cloth.

They had their tea in the kitchen while the curtains turned in the machine. Martin read the instructions on a packet of biscuits, folding the empty end underneath.

“Your sister rang,” he said.

“When?”

“Yesterday. I meant to say.”

Elsie waited, but there was nothing more to the message. She rang her sister after washing up. They spoke about a neighbour’s operation and whether it was too early to cut back roses. From the hall she could hear Martin moving his chair nearer the television.

The curtains were still damp at bedtime. She hung them over the banister with a towel beneath the hem. During the night Martin got up and caught his sleeve in one of the folds.

In the morning he mentioned it.

“I’ll put them back today,” she said.

“No hurry.”

But he fetched the steps before leaving for work. They stood open in the spare room all morning, and when Elsie carried the curtains upstairs she had to put them down again to move the steps nearer the window.

---

**hop 90**

In March the catch on Martin’s briefcase broke. He brought it into the kitchen and showed Elsie how the lid sprang open unless he kept his thumb against it.

“There’s a place by the station,” she said.

“I know.”

For several mornings he carried his papers in a blue shopping bag. Elsie put his sandwiches on top, wrapped twice so that the butter wouldn’t mark anything. When the briefcase came back, the new catch was brighter than the old one. He tried it three or four times while she cleared the breakfast things.

That Saturday they went to buy a lampshade. The one beside his chair had developed a brown patch, which showed only when the light was on. In the shop Martin held up two cream shades and asked her which she preferred.

“They’re much the same.”

“This one’s washable.”

“Then that one.”

They carried it home between them in a bag too large for either to hold comfortably. At the crossing he took her elbow, then let go when the traffic stopped.

Later she found the receipt on the hall table. On its blank side he had written a telephone number in pencil. She put it beneath the brass dish where they kept spare keys.

“Was that something you needed?” she asked when he was looking through the dish on Sunday.

“Just a number from work.”

He folded the receipt into his wallet. She had been going to mention the guarantee, but the shade was already fitted and there seemed little that could go wrong with it.

---

**hop 89**

At the beginning of April, Martin stopped taking sandwiches. Elsie had bought the ham already, so she put it between slices of bread for her own lunch and ate standing by the kitchen window.

“Are they doing something there now?” she asked that evening.

“What?”

“For lunch.”

“Oh. Sometimes we go out.”

She folded the empty bread bag and put it in the drawer with the others. He had always said there was nowhere decent near the office.

A week later he asked if they still had the small suitcase. There was a course in Birmingham, two nights, though in the end he came back after one. He said the second day wasn’t relevant to his department. Elsie unpacked a clean shirt and hung it in the wardrobe without asking about the first.

He began getting home before her on Thursdays. She would find the kettle warm and the letters opened, each envelope slit neatly along the top. Once he had taken the washing in, although the towels were still damp at the corners.

“You needn’t do those,” she said.

“It looked like rain.”

She spread them over the kitchen chairs. During supper he sat with his chair pulled forward, avoiding the towel behind him.

At the end of the month there was a dinner for someone who was leaving. Martin wore his good grey suit. Elsie asked whether wives were going.

“Not this one.”

She brushed a pale thread from his shoulder. When he came home, she was still awake, but neither of them switched on the light.

---

**hop 88**

In May, Martin brought home the mug Elsie had given him when they changed the name of the firm. It had his initials on one side and a blue band round the rim. She found it wrapped in newspaper at the bottom of his shopping bag.

“Has the handle gone?” she asked.

“Nothing wrong with it.”

There was a brown mark inside which she tried to remove with salt. When that failed, she stood it in bleach overnight. In the morning it looked almost new, except for a little scratch through the M.

She put it on the shelf beside their other mugs. Martin continued to use the white one.

They had been meaning to have the back bedroom painted. There were sample cards tucked behind the clock, and a man Elsie knew from the library had said his brother could do it reasonably. She mentioned this while Martin was watching the weather.

“I could do that,” he said.

“You’ve been saying.”

“Well, I could.”

She waited until the forecast had finished before asking when she should get the paint.

“No rush.”

The room was mostly used for drying washing. Elsie went up the next afternoon and moved the clothes horse away from the wall. Behind it, the paper had lifted at the join. She pressed it flat with her palm, but it curled out again as soon as she took her hand away.

For a few days she left the sample cards on his place at the table. Then she put them back behind the clock.

---

**hop 87**

The letter had come in June, though Martin said there was nothing in it he hadn’t known already. He read it at the kitchen table and put it back in the envelope, using the handle of his teaspoon to press down the flap.

“Do you need to answer it?” Elsie asked.

“No.”

She left it beside the telephone for a week. Then, when she needed to dust, she put it in the drawer with the instruction books and the spare batteries.

Through July he went out at the usual time. Elsie heard his key turn while she was still upstairs, and sometimes she came down to find his cup in the sink with the teabag squeezed against the side. He had never used the little dish she kept there for that purpose.

One Saturday they went into town for a new watch strap. The man behind the counter asked whether Martin wanted leather again.

“It gets knocked about,” Martin said.

Elsie looked at the watches in the cabinet. Each rested on a small velvet cushion, showing a different time.

On the bus home she asked whether there would be a presentation.

“Shouldn’t think so.”

“They usually do something.”

“Depends who’s collecting.”

He held the paper bag on his knee, although the old strap was all that was in it.

That Sunday Elsie cleaned the cupboard under the stairs. She threw out two dried tins of polish and a single glove. There were things of his at work, she supposed, that would have to go somewhere.

---

**hop 86**

On Monday Martin brought his overalls home in a carrier bag. Elsie found them beside the washing machine, folded with the sleeves tucked in.

“Won’t you want these?” she asked.

“Got another pair there.”

She washed them anyway. There was a receipt in the breast pocket, rubbed almost blank, and a screw she put on the kitchen windowsill. When the overalls were dry she hung them in the cupboard under the stairs. His good coat had to be moved along to make room.

They had not decided what to do about lunches. For years Elsie had eaten something standing up, often while the iron warmed or the radio gave the weather. On Tuesday she bought a larger loaf, then put half of it in the freezer.

“Don’t start cooking dinners,” he said.

“I wasn’t going to.”

“I can see to myself.”

“I know you can.”

On Thursday evening he polished his shoes over a newspaper. She sat opposite with the television listings open on her knee. Neither of them was watching the programme she had chosen.

“Will they want you back at all?” she asked.

“What for?”

“If someone’s off. Or they get busy.”

He worked the brush around the heel.

“They’ve had a lad in since Easter.”

She turned a page. There was something about gardens on in the afternoon, a repeat of a series they had missed.

Before bed she made his sandwiches and wrapped them in foil. She put the mustard back in the fridge, then took it out again to wipe the shelf beneath it.

---

**hop 85**

On Friday he came home twenty minutes earlier than usual. Elsie was upstairs changing the bed and heard him try the kitchen radio, then switch it off before anyone spoke.

“They let you go, then,” she said when she came down.

“Nothing to stay for.”

He had put his bag by the back door. Beside it stood a bottle in a cardboard sleeve and a small white envelope with his name written across it.

“Was there a speech?”

“Graham said a few words.”

She waited, holding the pillowcases against her waist.

“Mostly about the loading bay. When the drains backed up.”

“You told me about that.”

“Yes.”

They had fish for tea. Martin went to fetch it, and Elsie warmed the plates. He brought back a portion of peas neither of them had asked for and counted his change onto the mantelpiece.

On Saturday he woke at the usual time. By half past six she could hear him downstairs, opening the drawer where they kept batteries and old keys. When she joined him, the kitchen clock lay face down on a tea towel.

“It’s been losing,” he said.

“Has it?”

“Few minutes every week.”

He fitted a battery and held the clock against his ear. She filled the kettle around his elbow.

In the afternoon they went to the supermarket. At the checkout Martin reached for the heavier carrier, but she had already lifted it into the trolley.

“We’ll need potatoes,” she said.

“We’ve got them.”

She looked beneath the newspaper in the trolley. He was right. She had chosen them herself.

---

**hop 84**

On Sunday Martin emptied his work bag onto the kitchen table. There were three pens, a tin of mints, and a folded letter Elsie had already read. He put the letter aside and shook the bag over the bin.

“Crumbs,” he said.

She was peeling potatoes at the sink. In the window, his reflection held the bag open and looked inside it again.

“Will they want that back?”

“It’s mine. Bought it myself.”

“I couldn’t remember.”

He wiped the lining with a damp cloth, then left it spread across a chair. The bag had once been black. Along the bottom seam it had faded to the colour of the dust he brought home on his cuffs.

Their daughter rang after lunch. She asked whether they had made any plans. Elsie said they might go away, although this was the first either of them had mentioned it.

“Somewhere warm,” their daughter said.

Martin, listening from his armchair, raised a hand towards the telephone. Elsie passed it to him.

“Not in the school holidays,” he said.

Later Elsie wrapped the remaining potatoes and put them in the fridge. There was enough beef for sandwiches, provided she cut it thinly. She wrote mustard on the shopping list and checked the cupboard, where an unopened jar stood behind the vinegar. She crossed it out.

Martin came in carrying his bag.

“Dry already.”

He returned the pens and the mints to their pockets. The letter stayed on the table until Elsie moved it to wipe underneath. Then she put it beside the fruit bowl.

---

**hop 83**

On Monday Elsie bought a smaller loaf. The usual one had been going dry before they reached the end, and she disliked finding half a dozen slices in the bread bin, each too stiff for anything but toast.

Martin noticed at supper.

“Different bread?”

“Same bread. Smaller.”

He turned the packet to look at the price, then folded the opening underneath it.

In the evening they watched a programme about a couple moving to the coast. The couple wanted room for visitors, though neither mentioned who would visit. Martin said the roof would need attention. Elsie thought the kitchen looked cold.

During the advertisements he went upstairs and brought down the little diary he kept beside his bed. He counted through several pages, touching each Monday with his thumb.

“Not long now,” he said.

“No.”

“I’ll have to sort out those tools.”

She moved her feet so he could reach the shelf beneath the television. He put the diary there, on top of an old road atlas. Neither of them had used the atlas since their daughter had shown them how to get directions on a telephone.

Before bed, Elsie checked the back door. Through the glass she could see the pale rectangle of the shed window. A watering can stood beneath it, tilted against the wall, where Martin had left it the previous autumn.

She nearly asked whether he meant the tools in the shed or the ones at work. Upstairs, the bathroom tap was running. She switched off the kitchen light and followed him.

---

**hop 82**

The letter confirming Martin’s retirement arrived on a Tuesday, though everyone had known the date since February. He read it twice and left it beside the fruit bowl, where Elsie found it under a banana beginning to spot.

“They’ve spelt your middle name wrong,” she said.

“They always have.”

She laid the letter flat. There was to be a presentation on his last afternoon. Partners were welcome, it said, provided numbers were given to the office beforehand.

“Do you want me to come?”

“Not much point. A few speeches.”

“All right.”

He picked up the banana, considered it, and put it back.

After he had gone, she moved the letter to the drawer where they kept guarantees. The toaster was no longer theirs, but its folded instructions were still there. She made room without throwing anything away.

She had planned to clean the cupboard beneath the stairs that morning. Instead she took everything out, swept around the gas meter, and returned the things in a different order. Martin’s walking boots went at the front. He had mentioned walking, once the weather settled.
