At four she sat down with her tea. The house made its usual small noises: the fridge stopped, a pipe knocked upstairs, someone wheeled a bin along the alley. She could distinguish their neighbours’ gates without looking. Number twelve dragged over the paving. Number sixteen shut with a chain.

Theirs had a loose catch. Martin lifted it as he closed the gate, but the metal still struck twice, the second sound fainter than the first.

---

**hop 81**

For thirty-eight years, Elsie had known when to put the kettle on by the sound of the side gate. Before that they had lived in a flat, and she had watched for his bus from the kitchen window. She could not remember deciding to stop watching.

During his final week at work, Martin began bringing things home in a supermarket bag: a mug, a pair of shoes he kept beneath his desk, a photograph of Elsie taken on a holiday neither of them had particularly enjoyed.

“You can throw that away,” she said.

“Nothing wrong with the frame.”

He put it on the hall table. In the photograph she was holding her cardigan closed against the wind. Behind her, half a ferry was leaving the picture.

On Thursday she went to the library and renewed a book she had not opened. The woman at the counter asked whether she was looking forward to having him home.

“It’ll be different,” Elsie said.

“You’ll get him doing all those jobs.”

“Yes.”

Walking back, she bought two cream slices. She ate hers standing at the kitchen counter, then washed the plate and put the other slice in the fridge.

Martin found it after supper.

“What’s this for?”

“Nothing. They looked nice.”

He divided it with a knife and brought her half on a saucer.

She nearly told him she had already had one. Instead she moved her newspaper aside.

That night he polished his shoes, including the pair from beneath his desk. He left all four on newspaper by the back door, their toes pointing into the kitchen.

---

**hop 80**

On his last Friday, Martin brought home a carriage clock in a box too large for it. There was also a card, signed by people whose handwriting Elsie could not distinguish from one another’s.

“Did they make a speech?” she asked.

“Brian said a few words.”

“What sort of words?”

“The usual.”

He took the clock out and stood it on the mantelpiece. Its brass handle lay flat against the top. Elsie lifted it, then let it fall carefully back into place.

“Does it need winding?”

“Battery.”

He sounded faintly disappointed, though he had complained for years about having to wind the old one.

They ate fish with the kitchen window open. Next door, someone was cutting timber, stopping after each length as though waiting to be told whether it was right.

“I could clear the shed next week,” Martin said.

“There’s no hurry.”

“No.”

She had spent the afternoon moving her sewing things from the small bedroom. He had mentioned putting a desk there, although there was nothing he needed a desk for yet. The sewing machine was now under their bed, where she would have to kneel to reach it.

After supper he washed and she dried. He put the roasting tin in the wrong cupboard, and she left it there until he went upstairs.

Before bed, Elsie checked the alarm.

“Won’t need that much longer,” she said.

Martin was loosening his watch strap.

“Best keep to a routine.”

She turned the alarm off for the weekend and set it down with its face towards him.

---

**hop 79**

The letter from the pension office arrived on a Saturday, folded so that the important figure lay precisely along a crease. Martin smoothed it with the side of his hand.

“Well,” he said.

Elsie looked up from the shopping list. “Is it enough?”

“It’s what they said it would be.”

She waited, but he folded the letter again and put it beneath the clock, where they kept things that required no immediate action and therefore remained for months.

At the supermarket he pushed the trolley while she chose potatoes. This was their usual arrangement, though that morning he followed her down an aisle they did not need.

“Coffee’s the other way,” she said.

“I know.”

They stood beside birthday candles and paper napkins. Somewhere a child was being told that wanting something was not a reason.

“Will you want lunch every day?” Elsie asked.

“I generally do.”

“I mean a proper one.”

He considered this with an attention she had hoped he might give to the larger question.

“A sandwich would do.”

That afternoon she found him in the garden, testing the greenhouse door. It opened six inches before catching on the frame.

“Ground’s moved,” he said.

“Has it?”

“Something has.”

She held the door while he crouched to inspect the bottom hinge. Beyond his shoulder, last year’s tomato strings hung from the roof, each ending in a small, careful knot.

On Sunday evening he polished his shoes over newspaper. Elsie laid out two cups for morning and moved his slightly to the left, making room beside it for the marmalade.

---

**hop 78**

For thirty-eight years, Martin had left the house at seven forty, and Elsie had waited until seven forty-two to turn the radio up. It was not that he disliked music. He simply believed breakfast required concentration.

Now there were three working days remaining, and she found herself worrying about the radio.

“What will you do in the mornings?” she asked on Monday.

“Whatever I like.”

“Yes, but what do you like?”

He examined the hinge of his lunchbox. “I expect I’ll remember.”

She had once known exactly what she would do when they had time. There were seed catalogues in a drawer, railway timetables folded inside an atlas, a recipe for something requiring six uninterrupted hours. Over the years, these had become less like plans than evidence of another household living quietly inside their own.

On Tuesday she cleared a shelf for whatever he would bring back from his office. She found two brass screws in a saucer and carried them outside to the greenhouse. The instructions had dissolved during an optimistic weekend in April. Martin said he could manage without them, but then his sister had fallen ill, and after that there had always been weather.

On Wednesday evening, he stood in the kitchen doorway while she dried a plate.

“They’re doing speeches tomorrow.”

“You’ll survive.”

“That’s what worries me. All that time afterward.”

She handed him the plate, though it was already dry.

“Put this away, then.”

He did, and stayed to help with the others. Neither mentioned retirement again that night. Before bed, Elsie checked the forecast: rain through Friday, clearing at the weekend.

**hop 77**

On Martin’s last Thursday at the water department, he brought home a cardboard box containing three mugs, a broken stapler, and the retirement plan he had written at twenty-seven.

Elsie unfolded the yellow sheet beside the sink.

“Buy a boat,” she read. “Learn Italian. Never set an alarm again.”

“The stapler was more realistic,” Martin said.

Outside, rain ticked against the little greenhouse they had never finished assembling. Its door leaned against the fence, still wearing the manufacturer’s blue protective film. Elsie looked at it while Martin searched the cupboards for biscuits.

“We could start with the door,” she said.

He followed her gaze. “That’s not on the list.”

“Neither am I.”

He turned, but she was smiling. She took his hand and flattened the retirement plan between their palms.

Later, after they had washed up, Elsie remembered a novel she had borrowed during their honeymoon. There had been an older couple in it, people who mistook freedom for an empty calendar until something small changed their minds. She could remember the final paragraph almost exactly, but not what had happened just before it.

Martin opened the writing assistant on their ancient laptop.

“Perhaps it can find the missing bit.”

Elsie dictated the paragraph, correcting herself twice. Then Martin asked for the passage immediately preceding it.

While the little cursor pulsed, he fetched a pencil and added a fourth ambition to the yellow sheet: Finish greenhouse with Elsie.

“There,” he said. “A revised edition.”

The laptop chimed. Elsie leaned forward to read the answer aloud.

She had thought there would be more to discuss. For years he had mentioned things he would do when he had the time: mend the gate, go through the photographs, take the bus out to the reservoir on a weekday. Now, if she brought up any of these, he said there was no hurry.

On the first Monday in January she watched him from the bedroom window. He stopped at the gate to pull on his gloves. One finger had gone inside itself, and he worked at it with his bag held between his knees. She stayed until he had turned the corner, then opened the window to air the room.

Her own mornings had a settled order. She could be back from the shops before the woman next door went out with her dog. On Thursdays she changed the sheets. She wondered whether she would still do that on Thursdays, then felt foolish for wondering.

At supper he said someone from personnel would be writing.

‘About the arrangements.’

She passed him the potatoes.

‘You'll have to let me know.’

That winter he had begun coming home on the earlier bus. There was less to finish, he said, now that the new man was taking things over. Sometimes he stood in the kitchen without removing his coat while she moved between the cooker and the sink.

In March she cleared a shelf in the little room. Most of what she threw away was hers: old catalogues, a broken radio she had meant to have repaired. She left his road atlas where it was.

He noticed on Sunday when he went looking for string.

‘What’s happened in here?’

‘I’ve made some space.’

He ran a finger along the bare shelf, then asked where she had put the string.

They had three wet weeks. Each morning she spread yesterday’s paper by the back door. He came in carefully, holding the door frame as he took off his shoes, and carried them through to the kitchen radiator. By morning there would be a little grit beneath them, which she swept up after he left.
