She turned the box over to find the address.

‘As long as this is clear.’

He watched her put it on the scales. When she told him the price, he took a coin purse from inside his coat and tipped the money into his hand.

After he left, Denise put on her jacket. She stood at the counter fastening a button that had almost come loose.

‘You ought to catch that before it goes,’ she said.

Denise pulled once at the thread.

‘It’s been like it since Christmas.’

She ate her sandwiches sitting beside the sorting shelves. Her husband had used the end of the loaf again. She folded the foil with the crumbs inside and put it back in her bag.

For a while nobody came in. The clock above the cards clicked loudly each time the minute changed.

---

**hop 53**

At two o’clock Denise came back with a bag from the chemist and a cheese roll she said she didn’t want. She put the roll beside the kettle. There was a dark patch on the paper where the butter had come through.

‘Help yourself,’ she said.

She had already eaten half of it, neatly, from one end.

The afternoon delivery was short of second-class stamps. They counted the sheets twice, then Denise went to find the number for the depot. Under the delivery book was a postcard of a harbour, sent by someone who had worked there before either of them. It had arrived in June. Nobody knew whether to take it down.

A woman asked for a form to renew her driving licence. When she was given it, she folded it into quarters.

‘Will they mind?’ she asked.

‘You’ll have to open it out to fill it in.’

The woman looked at the creases, then put the form in her handbag.

Outside, a council van had stopped with two wheels on the pavement. The passenger stayed in his seat while the driver examined the litter bin. He lifted its flap and let it fall. Both men were still there when she went through to wash her hands.

The hot tap needed turning further than she expected. Water struck the basin and spotted her blouse.

When she came back, Denise handed her the new price sheet.

‘Those need doing before we forget.’

She found a pen that worked and carried the sheet to the display by the window.

---

**hop 52**

The rain began while she was changing the price on the padded envelopes. There were three sizes, but the new sheet listed four. She left the largest where it was and wrote the other prices on the backs of the old cards.

Denise was on the telephone to someone about her mother’s boiler.

‘She can’t keep doing that,’ she said. ‘She’s eighty-three.’

A man brought in a parcel wrapped in wallpaper, the patterned side turned in. He had written the address across a seam. She put clear tape over it, although he said the paper was stronger than it looked.

‘It’s only shoes,’ he said.

After he left, she found his shopping list beside the scales. Milk, pears, something crossed out so thoroughly that the paper had worn through. She put it beneath the counter in case he came back.

Denise finished her call and stood looking at the telephone.

‘They’re coming Thursday now.’

‘Is that any good?’

‘Better than Friday.’

At half past three the schoolchildren passed the window, some running, some stopping to wait for the others. A girl pressed her palm against the glass while she pulled up her sock. The mark stayed after she had gone.

She took the cloth from beside the sink, but it smelled of yesterday’s milk. She rinsed it twice and hung it over the tap.

There had been no proper lunch break. Earlier, she had moved her bag off the spare chair for a customer and put it behind the cartons. Now she could not remember whether she had brought anything.

---

**hop 51**

At four o’clock she found the sandwich in her bag. The bread had gone soft along the edge where the tomato was, and she ate the other half standing beside the kettle.

Denise came through carrying the stapler.

‘Have we got the ones for this?’

She opened the drawer beneath the tea bags. There were rubber bands in a saucer, two batteries and a packet of birthday candles, but no staples.

‘Try under the till.’

‘I did.’

They had been using the stapler all week without any staples in it. She thought of the notices she had put up on Monday, their corners tucked into the frame of the corkboard. That explained one thing.

The delivery driver arrived while she was washing her hands. He left two boxes by the entrance and asked Denise to sign on the screen. His sleeve had made a wet patch across the top box.

‘That one’s split underneath,’ he said, once she had signed.

She brought some packing tape from the counter. Between them they turned the box over, holding its flaps together. A roll of labels fell out and travelled beneath the chair where customers sat to address parcels.

‘Leave it,’ Denise said. ‘I’ll get it.’

But she was already down on one knee. There was a glove under the chair too, small and grey, with the thumb pushed inside. She set it on the windowsill where someone might see it from outside.

Water was collecting along the entrance mat. She went to the cupboard for something to wipe it with.

---

**hop 50**

The rain had stopped, though people were still coming in with their umbrellas open and shaking them just inside the door. She wiped the tiles with a handful of paper towels. There was a mop in the cupboard, but getting it out meant moving the boxes of printer paper.

Denise was checking a delivery against the invoice.

‘We didn’t order green,’ she said.

‘Put it with the others.’

‘There aren’t any others. That’s why we didn’t order it.’

She looked over. Six rolls of green raffle tickets lay on the counter, each held shut with a small piece of tape. Denise put a question mark beside them on the invoice and carried the box into the back.

A woman came in wanting a padded envelope large enough for a photograph. She held her hands apart to show the size, then brought them closer together.

‘It’s in a frame,’ she said.

They tried the photograph against three envelopes before the woman decided to take it home and find a box. She bought a book of second-class stamps instead. When she had gone, there was a damp rectangle on the counter where she had rested the frame.

Denise returned with her cardigan on.

‘Shall I do the door?’

‘Give it another minute.’

Outside, the man from the shoe repair shop was bringing in his pavement board. One hinge had worked loose, and he held the two halves together against his chest. She watched him back through his doorway, then pressed the button to open the till. The drawer struck her hip.

---

**hop 49**

At six, Denise turned the sign in the door while she finished counting the till. There was a pound missing. She counted the coins again, making little stacks on the rubber mat, and found it underneath a receipt.

‘There,’ she said.

Denise was pulling the blind down over the window display. It always caught halfway, where the cord had been knotted, and needed to be let up a little before it would come any further.

The last customer had left a basket beside the counter. In it were a packet of envelopes and a birthday card with no price on the back. She put the envelopes away but left the card by the till for the morning. A dog wearing spectacles looked up from the front of it.

‘Did you ring about the strip light?’ Denise asked.

‘They said Thursday.’

‘They said that last week.’

She folded the receipt around the notes and put them in the cash bag. Above the stationery shelves, the light gave a faint click without coming on. She had stopped noticing the dark patch during the day, but now the rest of the shop looked too bright around it.

Denise fetched the rubbish from behind the counter. The bag was scarcely half full.

‘Leave it,’ she said. ‘There’s nothing in there that’ll smell.’

But Denise had already tied the handles together.

She carried the cash bag into the office and locked it away. When she came back, the shop lights were off and the rubbish stood beside the delivery entrance, its thin plastic handles stretched white.

---

**hop 48**

Denise was looking for the key to the back door. It was supposed to hang beside the fuse box, but someone had put a delivery note over the hook.

‘You go,’ she said. ‘I’ll find it.’

She took her coat from the peg. One sleeve had turned inside out, and she stood with her hand in it for a moment, working the lining down. Through the wall she could hear Denise opening drawers.

There was a message on her phone. It had arrived at half past four, while she was kneeling beside the bottom shelf with a pencil behind her ear.

Do we have potatoes?

She had answered yes without thinking. Now she wasn’t sure. There had been two in the vegetable drawer that morning, one soft at the end, unless he had used them yesterday. She put the phone away.

In the staff kitchen she rinsed her cup and left it upside down on the draining board. Someone had taken home the tea towel. A wet circle spread beneath the rim.

‘Got it,’ Denise called.

They went out through the delivery entrance. Denise tested the door twice, then lifted her collar and headed towards the car park. She watched until Denise had found her car, although there was no reason to wait.

The alley brought her out beside the bank. She could turn left here and catch the bus for three stops, or walk past the shops. She checked the time on the bank’s illuminated sign. It was seven minutes slow, as it had been all summer.

She turned towards the shops.

---

**hop 47**

At the crossing she waited through a green light because an ambulance was coming, though it was still some distance away. The man beside her stepped out and then came back. Neither of them looked at the other.

She had stayed late to help count the stock. There were six boxes missing from the list and four on the shelf that nobody could account for. In the end Denise had drawn a line beneath the figures and said they could sort it out tomorrow.

Now the shops were shutting. Outside the chemist, a boy was dragging the stand of special offers backwards through the door. One wheel had caught on the mat.

She remembered they needed soap. She could have gone in; the boy was holding the door with his shoulder. Instead she walked on, feeling in her pocket for the receipt she had meant to throw away at work.

It had been three weeks since the telephone call. At first they had mentioned it each evening, usually while one of them was washing up. Then there had been several days when neither had said anything. On Sunday he had brought the biscuit tin down from the wardrobe and dusted the lid with his sleeve before putting it back.

At their gate she stopped to pull a twig from the shopping bag caught in the hedge. The bag tore, leaving a strip she couldn't reach.

The kitchen light was on. She let herself in carefully, holding the handle down until the latch was clear.

---

**hop 46**

He had put the oven on before she came home. From the hallway she could hear the fan, and the loose catch on the kitchen window ticking whenever the wind caught it.

‘There was something for you,’ he said.

She took off her coat and hung it over his, then lifted it away again so she could reach the hook underneath.

‘For me?’

‘For both of us, really.’

The envelope was lying where she usually put her keys. He had opened it along the short edge with a knife, leaving the flap intact. She pulled out the folded sheet and stood beside the table to read it.

He went on scraping a potato. There was a dark place near one end which he worked at with the point of the peeler.

‘Tuesday,’ she said.

‘Yes.’

‘You’ve got the man coming about the gutter.’

‘I can ring him.’

She read the last paragraph again. It asked them to bring identification and any relevant documents. She wasn't sure whether the things in the biscuit tin counted as documents. Some were only copies, and one had been written on the back of a shopping list.

‘We ought to get everything together.’

‘After we’ve eaten.’

She folded the sheet along its old creases, then opened it and laid it flat. He tipped the potatoes into the saucepan, keeping one hand across the rim to hold back the peelings.

‘Fish all right?’

‘Yes. Lovely.’

She washed her hands at the sink. The soap had become a thin piece that slipped between her fingers. Behind her, he opened the oven and set the timer for another five minutes.

---

**hop 45**

The letter was still on the kitchen table, though they had both read it. She moved it when he brought the plates through, then moved it back beside the salt.

‘Does it say which entrance?’ he asked.

‘On the back. There’s a map.’

He turned it over. The little arrows led from a road they knew to a building neither of them recognised.

‘That used to be the car park.’

‘Perhaps it still is underneath.’

They ate the fish while it was hot. He had done too many potatoes, and towards the end of the meal he offered her the bowl again without looking up.

‘No, thank you.’

‘They’ll keep.’

She collected the plates while he found a container. There were lids in the drawer that no longer fitted anything, and he tried two before she reached past him for the right one.

Outside, someone was pulling a wheelie bin over the paving. She went to the window, but it was already too dark to see beyond their own reflection.

‘Is ours out?’

‘Not till Thursday.’

‘Of course.’

He ran the hot tap and waited with his fingers under it. She took a tea towel from the handle of the oven.

‘You don’t have to dry.’

‘I might as well.’

He washed the saucepan first, turning it under the water to inspect the rim. She dried it and put it in the cupboard. Then came the bowls, the knives, and the small sieve he had used for the peas. They left that on the draining board.

---

**hop 44**

After washing up he went outside to check that the car would start. She heard the engine turn over, stop, then turn over again. When he came in he put the keys in the blue dish by the telephone instead of in his coat.

‘Petrol’s all right,’ he said.

She was folding the tea towel along its damp crease.

‘We won’t need to leave till seven.’

‘I know. Just checking.’

They watched the end of a programme neither of them had chosen. A man was showing a young couple where a wall could be taken down. He stood to one side so that they could imagine the space without it.

‘Would you like anything?’ he asked when the news came on.

She looked at the clock.

‘There’s time for tea.’

He made it in the pot, and they drank from the good cups because the others were in the dishwasher. Afterwards she carried hers into the kitchen and rinsed it. She left his on the arm of his chair; he still had some.

Upstairs, she checked the small bag once more. Slippers, a book, the case for her glasses. She took the book out and put in another, thinner one.

He called up to ask where she had put the alarm clock.

‘Your side,’ she called back.

When he came to bed she was lying with her back to the door. He set the alarm, testing the little switch with his thumbnail. Then he turned off the lamp.

For a while they could hear a television in the house next door. Neither spoke. Eventually it stopped.

---

**hop 43**

She woke before the alarm and lay listening to the cistern fill. He had been into the bathroom twice during the night, taking care each time not to close the door too firmly.

The envelope was on the dressing table beneath her hairbrush. She had read the instructions again before bed, though there was nothing in them she had not understood the first time. No food. No drink. Someone to collect her afterwards.

He came back into the bedroom.

‘Is it time?’ she asked.

‘Not yet. Half past six.’

He sat down on his side and reached for his socks. She watched him feel along the carpet for the second one.

‘Under the chair,’ she said.

He found it and shook it once, as if something might have got inside.

Yesterday she had bought milk and a small loaf. There was soup in the freezer, in containers she had labelled when she still thought labelling things would save explanations. She had told him about the soup while they were washing up.

‘I can manage a lunch,’ he had said.

‘I know you can.’

Now he pulled the curtain back a little. The glass showed him more clearly than it showed the garden.

‘Looks wet.’

She got up after he went downstairs. In the bathroom she washed her face, then dried the basin with the corner of her towel. Her nightdress went beneath the pillow. She dressed slowly, leaving her watch until last because the clasp was awkward and there was no reason to hurry yet.

---

**hop 42**

At seven he came upstairs carrying a cup of tea, then stopped in the doorway.

‘You’re not allowed this, are you?’

‘Not since midnight.’

He looked into the cup as though there might be something else in it. She was sitting on the edge of the bed, fastening her watch.

‘Have it yourself,’ she said.

Downstairs, he drank the tea standing up. She went from room to room turning off lights, although it was already light outside. In the small bedroom she straightened the cover on the sewing machine. There was a pair of his trousers underneath it, pinned at one ankle.

‘We ought to leave,’ he called.

She put on the coat she wore for shopping. The better one had a loose button, and she did not want to be thinking about a button all morning.

At the front door he asked whether she had everything. She touched her handbag, feeling the stiff edge of the hospital envelope through the lining.

‘Yes.’

He locked up while she waited by the car. Their neighbour’s recycling box was out a day early, the cardboard darkened by rain. She wondered whether to move it under the hedge, but he was coming down the path with the keys in his hand.

The car windows were misted on the inside. He wiped a circle with his sleeve before switching on the fan.

‘Cold?’

‘No.’

At the first lights, she noticed that he had buttoned his cardigan unevenly. She almost told him. Instead she watched a cyclist pull up beside them and put one foot on the wet road.

---

**hop 41**

They had left the bag on the kitchen table. She remembered it as they turned into the hospital road: the zip open, the sleeves of her nightdress folded over the top. Beside it were the slippers he had bought the previous Saturday, still joined by a length of plastic.

‘We’ll have to go back,’ he said.

‘You can go.’

He drove past the entrance before finding somewhere to stop. There were yellow lines along the kerb and a sign about keeping access clear. He left the engine running.

For a moment neither of them unfastened a seat belt. The dashboard clock was four minutes fast. She had asked him to change it in the spring, and since then had learned to make the adjustment without thinking.

‘There’s plenty of time,’ he said.

She took the letter from her handbag and checked the building name again. At home she had read all three pages, including the instructions about jewellery. Her wedding ring was in a saucer beside the sink.

‘Bring my glasses too. The ones by the bed.’

‘Have you not got them?’

‘These are the other ones.’

He looked towards the entrance. A man in blue was holding the automatic doors open for someone with a walking frame.

‘I could see you in first.’

She opened her door, and the sound the engine made was suddenly louder.

‘It’s only a desk,’ she said.

He waited while she got out. She bent to tell him where the slippers were, then remembered they were with the bag. Instead she said she would keep her phone on.

---

**hop 40**

At reception they asked whether anyone had come with her. She said her husband was parking, though by then he would have gone home for the things. It seemed easier than explaining why she had sent him away.

The woman behind the desk gave her a wristband and watched while she fastened it. She made it too loose. When she turned her hand, the printed part slipped underneath.

‘We can change that upstairs,’ the woman said.

A porter took her along a corridor where the floor changed from grey to green. She could have walked, but he had already brought the chair. Her coat lay across her knees with one sleeve trailing near the wheel. At the lift he stopped to tuck it in.

‘Don’t want to lose that,’ he said.

She thanked him, although she had been thinking for months of buying another.

The bed was beside a window. Someone had left a packet of tissues in the locker and a paper cup on the sill. She put the cup in the bin, then wondered whether it had been there for a reason.

A nurse drew the curtain and asked her to change. There were two ties on the gown, neither where she expected. She managed the lower one and held the neck closed until the nurse returned.

‘Let me get that.’

She stood still while the knot was made. Afterwards she climbed into bed without being asked. The nurse raised the rail, checked the wristband and wheeled over a trolley with a small tray on top.

---

**hop 39**

The nurse asked which arm she preferred. She held out the left, then remembered the bruise and offered the other.

‘Either’s fine,’ the nurse said.

Outside the window was a flat roof with water gathered around a drain. A plastic chair stood beside a door that had no handle on this side. She wondered who was allowed to sit there.

Her husband arrived carrying the blue shopping bag. He had brought slippers, a clean nightdress and the long charger from behind the television. The slippers were the pair she kept for visitors.

‘Were these the ones?’ he asked.

She said they were.

He set them under the bed, pointing outwards, and folded the bag into a square. There was nowhere obvious to put it. He held it on his knees until she told him about the locker.

‘Did you speak to anyone?’

‘They said someone would come round.’

He looked towards the doorway, as if the person might already be there, waiting for him to finish.

She had been given a form with her name printed incorrectly at the top. It was only one letter. When she pointed it out, he took a pen from his jacket and corrected it, pressing hard against the paper on the bed table.

‘That should do it,’ he said.

For a while they watched a man across the corridor trying to close a window. He pulled at the handle with both hands before a cleaner showed him the catch. Her husband smiled. She moved her feet beneath the blanket to make room for him to sit closer.

---

**hop 38**

Her husband had stayed until the woman in the next bed began receiving visitors. There were three of them, and they stood close together while one searched in a bag for something she had brought.

‘I’ll let you get some rest,’ he said.

He put the charger in the locker, though her telephone was at home. Neither of them mentioned this. Then he moved the chair back against the wall.

She asked whether he had eaten.

‘There was something in the fridge.’

She knew what he meant: a plate covered with another plate, on the middle shelf. She had left it there before going out. It seemed worth telling him that the smaller plate should not go in the dishwasher, but he was already bending to kiss her.

Later someone brought tea. She held the cup by its handle and watched a pale skin form at the edge. Beyond the curtain, the visitors discussed where they had parked.

During the night she woke several times. Once she thought she heard her husband opening a drawer. It was a nurse taking a thermometer from the cupboard beside the door.

‘Sorry,’ the nurse said, although she had made very little noise.

There was something she wanted to ask the doctor in the morning. She put the question into a sentence and repeated it, making the sentence shorter each time. She considered writing it on the paper beside her bed, but the pen was in her bag.

By six the corridor lights were on. A trolley stopped outside, then moved away again.

---

**hop 37**

She had been awake when they came to change the water in the jug. The girl who brought it asked if she wanted the blind raised, and she said halfway, although there was nothing opposite except another part of the hospital.

Her shoes were under the bed. Someone had put them together with the toes pointing out, ready for her to step into. She watched the girl straighten a corner of the sheet before leaving.

At nine a doctor came. He sat on the chair rather than standing beside the bed. She noticed that his identification card had turned round, showing a strip of white paper tucked into the plastic holder.

He asked what she remembered.

‘Most of it,’ she said.

He explained something again. She understood the separate words, and when he paused she nodded. There was a question she had thought of during the night, but now she could remember only that it had seemed important.

Afterwards she dressed behind the curtain. Her skirt fastened more easily than she expected. She checked the locker twice, taking out a packet of tissues and then the charger her husband had brought the evening before.

The telephone was near the lifts. She dialled home and listened to her own recorded voice inviting her to leave a message.

‘It’s me. They say I can come home.’

She waited, then added the name of the ward.

Back in the room, she sat on the chair. The bed had already been stripped. She kept her bag on her lap so it would not be in anyone’s way.

---

**hop 36**

The nurse asked whether there was anyone she would like them to telephone. She said her husband was already on his way, then remembered that she had only left a message at the house.

‘He’ll be here soon,’ she said.

The nurse brought her a cup of tea. It was too hot to hold without the saucer, so she set it on the windowsill. Below, a man in a brown jacket was helping someone reverse a car. He kept raising one hand and stepping backwards.

There were papers to collect, but not until Monday. The woman at the desk wrote down the number she should ring and circled the extension.

‘After ten is usually best.’

She folded the paper and put it in her purse, behind a receipt she had meant to throw away.

Her husband arrived carrying her scarf. He had found it on the kitchen chair and thought she might need it. While he spoke to the nurse, she went back for the tea. A pale skin had formed across it. She carried the cup to the desk, looking for somewhere to leave it.

In the car park he asked whether she wanted to go straight home.

‘We need milk.’

‘I got some yesterday.’

She nodded. He put the hospital bag at her feet, where it tipped against her shoes at each corner.

At home she took off her coat. There was a mark on one cuff, perhaps from the tea. She rubbed it with her thumb while her husband opened the curtains.

---

**hop 35**

For three days the bag from the hospital stood beside the telephone. Her husband had offered to unpack it, but she said there was washing in it and she would do it with the towels.

On Wednesday she carried it upstairs. The handles had been tied together, and she worked at the knot rather than cut it. Inside were her mother’s slippers, a hairbrush with several white hairs caught beneath the bristles, and the nightdress they had bought in a hurry. Someone had folded the nightdress with the sleeves crossed over the front.

She put it in the laundry basket. Then she took it out again to check the label.

Downstairs, the telephone rang. It was the woman from next door to her mother, asking whether the dustbins should still be put out.

‘There won’t be much in them,’ she said.

‘No, but I thought I’d ask.’

She thanked her. After they had finished speaking, she stayed by the telephone with the hairbrush in her hand.

Her husband came home with bread. He had bought the sliced kind, although they usually bought a loaf, and he explained that the bakery had been closed.

‘That’s all right,’ she said.

They ate eggs on toast. Afterwards he washed the plates while she dried them and put them away. There was a saucepan soaking on the hob from the previous evening. She changed the water in it, added a little washing-up liquid and left it to soak again.

Before bed she hung her coat on the banister, ready to take to the cleaner’s.

---

**hop 34**

On Thursday she found a key in the pocket of the coat she had worn to the hospital. It was attached to a square of blue plastic, with nothing written on it. She put it on the kitchen windowsill while she emptied the other pocket: a parking ticket, two peppermints, a tissue folded into quarters.

At first she thought it belonged to the back door. Then she remembered the back door key was longer, and that her mother had kept it on a hook beside the gas meter.

‘Could be the garage,’ her husband said.

‘There isn’t a garage.’

‘The shed, then.’

She rubbed a mark on the plastic with her thumbnail. Her mother had given her the key during one of the earlier visits, before anyone had thought to bring clothes for more than a night. There had been something she wanted fetched. A cardigan, perhaps, though a cardigan would not have been locked away.

She left the key where it was.

The following afternoon she walked to the shops without a list and came home with potatoes, beans and a packet of biscuits they already had. She put the biscuits behind the open packet in the cupboard. Then she washed the potatoes and cut away the dark places, dropping the pieces onto a sheet of newspaper.

Outside, her husband was bringing in the washing. One of his shirts had caught on the rose bush. Through the window she watched him free the sleeve carefully before folding it over the basket.

The key was still beside the little pot of chives. She moved it away from a splash of water.

---

**hop 33**

Her sister rang on Friday evening while she was draining the potatoes. She set the saucepan in the sink and answered with the tea towel still over her shoulder.

‘I’ve done the airing cupboard,’ her sister said. ‘Most of it can go to the charity shop. Unless you want any towels.’

‘No. I don’t think so.’

‘There are some good ones.’

She could hear a cupboard closing at the other end, then the sound of her sister moving through the house. Neither of them had stayed there overnight since the funeral, but her sister had begun taking her lunch in a plastic box so she could work through the afternoon.

‘Leave them for now,’ she said.

Her husband came into the kitchen carrying two plates. She pointed to the drawer for the cutlery.

‘What about the papers?’ she asked.

‘Still in the little desk. I haven’t touched those.’

They agreed she would go over the next day. Her sister had an appointment in the morning and might come later. There were bags under the sink, she said, enough for whatever they decided to throw away.

After the call, she put the potatoes back on the hob to dry. Her husband had served the beans and was waiting for her to sit down.

‘You don’t have to finish it this weekend,’ he said.

‘I know.’

Later she made a shopping list on the back of an envelope. She checked the milk, then stood beside the open fridge trying to remember what else she had meant to write.

---

**hop 32**

On Saturday morning she went to the supermarket first. They needed milk at home, and she had promised to get the bread her husband liked. In the household aisle she stopped beside the rolls of bin liners. Her sister had said there were plenty, but she put a packet in the trolley anyway.

At the checkout she found the list in her coat pocket. Under milk and bread she had written solicitor, though there was nothing she could do about that until Monday.

She took the shopping home and put away the things that needed the fridge. Her husband was in the garden, tipping rainwater out of the wheelbarrow.

‘Will you be back for lunch?’ he asked through the open door.

‘Probably not.’

He nodded and turned the wheelbarrow on its side against the fence. She left the bread on the counter where he would see it.

The road to the house was being resurfaced near the shops. Waiting at the temporary lights, she noticed that the newsagent had become a place that repaired phones. Her mother had still called it the paper shop, long after she stopped having a newspaper delivered.

She parked opposite the house because a skip occupied the space outside. It belonged to the neighbours. There were broken tiles in it and a white cupboard door with its handle attached.

Before getting out she checked her phone. No message. She put the bin liners under her arm and crossed between the parked cars, pressing the keys into her palm to separate the one she needed.

---

**hop 31**

Her sister was already upstairs when she arrived. There was a bag of coat hangers in the hall and a square of darker wallpaper where the little mirror had been. She set her keys on the telephone table, then picked them up again.

In the front bedroom the curtains were still drawn. Her sister had tried the cord, but something inside the track had stuck.

‘We could leave them,’ she said.

‘They’ll want everything out.’

She fetched the kitchen stool. One of its rubber feet was missing, so her sister held the back while she reached up. The hooks came free more easily when she lifted the weight of the fabric. At the last one she had to change hands.

They laid the curtains across the bed. Without them, the window looked larger and rather dirty. There was a dead fly on the sill, which her sister brushed into an empty envelope.

‘Would they do for your spare room?’

She said she wasn’t sure of the width.

Downstairs they drank tea from mugs her sister had brought in a shopping bag. The kettle would be going too, but not until the last day. She rinsed her mug and left it upside down on the draining board.

They carried the curtains to her car together, folded loosely between them. Her sister tucked in a corner before she closed the boot.

That evening she took them into the spare room and put them on the chair. She moved a stack of magazines to make space, but left the window as it was.

---

**hop 30**

On Saturday she found a curtain hook in the pocket of her cardigan. She had been looking for a tissue and brought it out with a little fluff caught in the curved end. For a while she could not place it. Then she remembered standing on the stool at her mother’s, passing the curtains down to her sister.

She put the hook in the saucer beside the cooker. There were two buttons there already, neither belonging to anything she wore.

The morning had been dry enough for washing. She stripped the bed and carried the sheets downstairs, holding the bundle high so that the ends would not trail. Outside, one of the clothes pegs broke in her hand. She put both pieces in her pocket and used another.

At lunchtime she ate bread and cheese standing at the counter. The neighbour was cutting his hedge. Each time the machine stopped she thought he had finished, but after a short interval it began again.

Her sister had sent a message asking about the curtains. She read it twice, then replied that she would look at the window tomorrow. The tape measure was in the car, unless she had left it behind.

Before bed she went out to bring in the sheets. They were cold and slightly damp along the hems. She folded them over her arm and paused beside the car, noticing a pale scrape near the boot handle. It came away when she rubbed it with her thumb.

She left the sheets over the banister overnight.

---

**hop 29**

On Sunday afternoon she cleared the things from the boot that had been there since the trip to her sister’s. There was a folding chair, a pair of shoes in a carrier bag and a road atlas with its cover bent backwards. She took the chair into the shed. The shoes she left on newspaper in the kitchen, although the mud on them had long since dried.

Her sister rang while she was shaking out the boot liner. She let the telephone ring, then went indoors and called back from the hall. They discussed the weather and whether their mother’s curtains would fit the spare room window. Neither had measured them.

‘There’s no hurry,’ her sister said.

Afterwards she stood for a moment with the receiver still in her hand, listening to the quiet line before putting it down.

She had meant to wash the car, but the light was going. Instead she wiped the inside of the windscreen with a cloth from under the sink. This left broad marks which showed when she leaned across to gather the parking receipts from the passenger footwell. Most were too faded to read. She checked each one before dropping it into the bin.

That evening she made a shopping list on the back of a circular. Potatoes, eggs, washing-up liquid. She opened the fridge, added milk, and put the list beside her keys.

Rain began as she was going upstairs. Through the landing window she could see it gathering on the car roof, bright beneath the streetlamp.

---

**hop 28**

The first time she noticed it was on Monday, coming out of the supermarket car park. A short scraping sound came from somewhere near her feet. She thought she had driven over a plastic bottle, but in the mirror there was nothing on the tarmac except a flattened cardboard box.

At home she put the shopping away and left the potatoes in their bag beside the back door. One of the eggs had cracked. She broke it into a cup, covered the cup with a saucer and put it on the top shelf of the fridge, where she would see it in the morning.

On Tuesday she walked to work. The car stayed outside the house with a yellow leaf caught under its rear wiper. She remembered the sound while waiting to cross by the chemist, then forgot it when a bus pulled in and blocked her view of the traffic.

After supper she went out to check the tyres. They looked much as they always did. She crouched beside the front wheel, holding her cardigan clear of the pavement, but could see nothing hanging loose. A neighbour passed with his dog and asked whether she had lost something. She said she was only looking.

Back inside, she found the invoice from the last service in the drawer beneath the cutlery. She copied the telephone number onto an envelope rather than take the whole sheet to work. Then she washed the saucepan and divided the remaining soup between two containers, leaving one to cool on the draining board.

---

**hop 27**

On Wednesday she took the car to work because rain was forecast. At the end of her road, turning left, she heard the noise again. It stopped when she straightened the wheel. She turned the radio down and listened until she reached the main road, but there was only the usual rattle from the things in the door pocket.

She had written the garage number on the back of an envelope. During her morning break she looked for it in her bag, taking out her purse and the case for her glasses before finding it caught inside a receipt. The line was busy the first time. When she tried again, a woman took her registration and said someone would call about an appointment.

At lunchtime it had still not rained. She ate outside on the low wall beside the car park, moving along when she noticed a damp patch under her coat. Someone had left a teaspoon on the wall. She carried it into the staff kitchen with her empty container and put it in the sink.

That evening she parked facing the other way so she would not have to turn sharply when she left in the morning. It meant walking a little farther with her bag. Indoors, she hung her coat over a chair and warmed the soup left from Tuesday.

Before bed she checked that her phone was charging. The cable had come loose from the socket behind the table. She pushed it in and waited for the small light to appear.

---

**hop 26**

On Thursday the garage rang while she was eating lunch. She let the call finish, then listened to the message with her sandwich still in her hand. They could take the car on Monday if she could leave it with them the night before. She called back and asked how late someone would be there. The man explained about the key slot beside the office door.

At work she wrote Monday on a piece of paper and put it in her coat pocket. When she found it that evening, she had forgotten what it was for. Then she remembered the noise the car made when she turned left, and set the paper on the kitchen counter.

There was enough milk for breakfast but not much else. She went to the shop after dinner and bought a small carton, bread, and a packet of biscuits that had been reduced because the box was crushed. At home she transferred the biscuits to a tin. Two were broken, and she ate those while putting the shopping away.

On Friday she changed the towels. The clean bath towel caught on the cupboard hinge, pulling a loop from the edge. She worked it back through with her fingernail, then folded it so the damaged part was underneath.

The bathroom mat needed washing too, but there were shirts already in the machine. She left it where it was and carried the towels downstairs. Before starting another load, she checked the pockets of her trousers. There was a pound coin in one and a folded shopping list in the other.

---

**hop 25**

On Saturday she washed the bathroom mat and hung it over the landing rail. The label said it could go in the dryer, but the rubber backing had begun to come away at one corner. By evening it was still damp underneath. She turned it over before going to bed.

Her sister called on Sunday morning to ask whether she wanted a small table. It had been in the spare room and was now in the hall, where everyone had to walk around it. She asked for the measurements, although she knew there was nowhere in her own house to put it. Her sister said she would find a tape measure and call back.

After the call she went outside to check the car. The appointment card from the garage was clipped behind the sun visor. She took it indoors, along with an empty water bottle and a receipt from the passenger footwell. The receipt had faded except for the total.

She found the bus timetable in a drawer beneath some takeaway menus. There were two services that would get her to work on time. She chose the earlier one and left the timetable beside her keys.

That evening she drove to the garage and put the keys through the slot, as they had arranged. She tried the flap afterwards to make sure they had dropped clear. Walking home, she remembered that her umbrella was still in the boot. The sky was clear, and she decided not to look for the other one until morning.

---

**hop 24**

On Monday she took the bus because the car was due for its service. The driver waited while she looked for the right money, then told her the fare had gone up. She put the extra coins in separately and kept the ticket in her hand all the way to the office.

At lunchtime the garage called. There was nothing wrong that needed doing immediately, but the front tyres would need watching. She wrote this on the back of an envelope. When she collected the car, the seat was farther back than she liked.

She stopped for bread on the way home and bought six rolls instead. Two went in the bread bin and the others in the freezer, still in their paper bag. Later she wondered whether she should have wrapped them in something else.

The letter about the meter was under a catalogue she had meant to throw away. It gave Wednesday as the day and asked that the cupboard be accessible. She read it standing by the stairs. There were shoes in the cupboard, and a carrier bag containing the attachments for a vacuum cleaner she no longer owned.

On Tuesday evening she carried the bag out to the bin. One of the narrow attachments fell through a split in the plastic. She picked it up and pushed it down beside the bag.

Back inside, she set her work shoes together on the bottom stair. One lace had worn almost through where it passed into the top hole.

---

**hop 23**

On Wednesday the man came to read the meter. She had moved the shoes from under the stairs before leaving for work, but when she came home there was a card on the mat saying he had been unable to gain access. She put the shoes back and left the card beside the telephone.

There was a number to call between eight and six. She underlined it with a pencil, although there were no other numbers on that side.

That evening she ate an omelette from the small plate because both large ones were in the dishwasher. It was not full enough to run. She rinsed the pan and stood it against the tiles, with a folded cloth underneath to catch the water.

Her sister rang while she was putting the milk away. They spoke about a road being closed near their mother's old house. Neither of them went that way now, but her sister had seen a photograph in the local paper. There was some disagreement about which corner the chemist had been on. After the call she found she had left the fridge door open.

Thursday was colder. At lunchtime she went into the shoe shop and asked whether they sold replacement laces. The assistant brought out a box from beneath the counter. She chose brown ones that looked a little too long.

At home she threaded one shoe, then the other, leaving the old laces coiled on the newspaper. Before bed she checked the forecast and brought her winter coat down from the wardrobe.

---

**hop 22**

On Friday she found a pair of gloves in the pocket of the coat she had meant to take to the cleaners. One was inside the other. She separated them and laid them on the hall table, where they stayed beside an envelope addressed to the previous tenant.

At work there had been a collection for someone on the floor above. She knew the name but could not remember whether he was retiring or going to another company. She put two pounds in the envelope and passed it to the next desk. Later, waiting for the kettle, she saw him carrying a plant towards the lift.

Saturday morning was dry enough to hang out the towels. She wiped the line with a damp cloth first. A peg came apart in her hand, and she kept the spring on the kitchen windowsill in case the other half turned up.

She made soup after lunch, using the carrots from the bottom drawer and an onion that had begun to soften. While it simmered she wrote a shopping list on the back of a letter about her house insurance. Tea, peas, washing-up liquid. She checked the bread bin and added bread, then crossed it out because there was half a loaf in the freezer.

By four the towels were still damp along the seams. She brought them in and arranged them over the airer in the spare room. The sheet beneath it was crooked. She straightened it with her foot before going downstairs to turn off the kitchen radio.

---

**hop 21**

On Sunday she took the shopping bags out of the cupboard and shook the crumbs from the largest one into the sink. A penny rolled under the draining board. She could see it through the rack but left it until she had put the dishes away.

The supermarket had moved the tea to the aisle with the biscuits. She went past it twice before asking a man who was opening a box of shelf labels. He pointed without standing up. She bought the smaller packet, although the larger one worked out cheaper, because there was still some in the tin at home.

Her sister rang while she was putting the frozen peas away. They talked about whether their mother needed a new television. There was nothing wrong with the picture, but the remote had become difficult to use. Her sister said she would try changing the batteries first.

On Monday a man came to test the alarms at the office. Each time one sounded, conversation stopped and then resumed a little louder. She had been asked to leave the cupboard beside her desk unlocked. When he had finished, she locked it again and put the key back in the drawer.

At home she warmed the rest of the soup and ate it with two slices of toast. The television listings were still folded to Saturday. She turned the page, then switched the set off before the programme she had chosen began. Before bed she checked that her bus pass was in her coat.

---

**hop 20**

On Tuesday the bus stopped two streets short of the office because of work being done to a drain. She followed the other passengers past the barriers. Someone had left a glove on top of a ticket machine, with the fingers tucked beneath it to keep it from blowing away.

There was an email about the kitchen fridge. Anything without a name would be thrown out on Friday. She went to look for the yoghurt she remembered bringing, but it wasn't there. Back at her desk she checked a delivery address against the one on an invoice. They were the same, except that one included the county.

At lunch she rang the dentist. The appointment card had been on her table for three weeks, propped against the salt. The receptionist offered her Thursday morning or a date in February. She said February would be better and wrote the new date on the back of an envelope.

That evening she put a wash on. While the machine filled, she emptied the pockets of the coat she had been wearing since October. There were two receipts, a tissue and the paper sleeve from a sandwich. She kept one receipt because she couldn't remember whether the shoes it was for had been comfortable.

The wash finished late. She arranged the heavier things nearest the radiator, moving a shirt to make room for the trousers. In the morning she turned everything over before leaving for work. A little water had gathered beneath one sleeve. She wiped it with her sock, then changed her socks.

---

**hop 19**

On Wednesday evening she took the washing off the rack, leaving the waistband of one pair of trousers spread over the bathroom rail. The rest was dry enough. She folded it on the bed while listening to a programme she had already heard, or one with the same people saying much the same things.

A bulb had gone in the hallway. She unscrewed it and put it in her bag so she could buy the right sort. Last time she had come home with two that wouldn't fit, and they were still in their packet beneath the sink.

At the supermarket she used a basket rather than a trolley. She needed bread, milk and something for lunches. The larger carton was cheaper by volume, according to the label, but she wasn't sure she would finish it. She stood with the fridge door open until someone behind her said excuse me.

Outside, she remembered the bulb. The hardware shop had closed at six.

When she got home there was a message from her sister asking about Christmas. She typed that she would have to check the dates, although the office would close for the same week it always did. Then she added a question about the children.

She made soup with the carrots that had begun to bend. While it simmered, she washed the container she would take to work and set it upside down on the draining board.

Before bed she checked her account. Her wages had gone in. For a moment the balance looked like money she could spend.

---

**hop 18**

The letter arrived on a Thursday, folded so that the amount showed through the window instead of her name. She could see what it was before opening it. The agent had mentioned a review in July, using a word that made it sound as though either of them might decide against it.

She left the envelope beside the kettle while she got ready for work. There was enough milk for tea if she didn't have cereal. She put two slices of bread in the toaster and held the milk carton up to the light.

On the bus she worked out the increase by the week. It was less alarming that way, though she knew this made no difference. A woman across the aisle was speaking to someone about a delivery. Apparently they had come to the wrong door again.

At lunchtime she opened a property website, then closed it when a colleague asked whether she wanted anything from the shop.

“I'm all right, thanks.”

She had brought soup in a container whose lid needed to be kept level.

That night she read the whole letter. The new rent would begin in November. She put it in the drawer with the instruction booklet for the washing machine and a spare key she had never tested.

Before going to bed, she went through the flat turning things off at the wall. The socket behind the television was difficult to reach. She knelt down and found it by touch, steadying herself with one hand on the skirting board.

---

**hop 17**

For two weeks the bathroom tap had needed an extra turn. She meant to tell the agent, but each morning she managed to stop it dripping and forgot about it until the next day.

She had lived in the flat for four years. There were still two boxes on top of the wardrobe, labelled in a thick black pen she no longer owned. One held Christmas decorations. She wasn't sure about the other. When she first moved in, she had planned to unpack everything before inviting anyone round.

On Monday the downstairs neighbour caught her by the bins and asked whether her heating was working.

“I haven't put it on,” she said.

“No, neither have I.”

They stood a moment longer, each holding something that belonged in a different bin.

That evening she ate at the small table beside the fridge. A screw had worked loose in one of the chairs, so she used the other and kept her bag on the bad one. Her father had offered to fix it at Christmas. She had said she would bring it over in the car, forgetting she no longer had a car.

After washing up, she checked her banking app. The balance was what she expected. She checked the pending payments as well, then put the phone face down.

On Wednesday she bought a basil plant reduced to forty pence. She took off its plastic sleeve over the sink and set it where it would get the morning light. It made the kitchen look cared for. Before bed, she wiped the soil from the draining board.

---

**hop 16**

The renewal arrived on a Thursday, attached to an email that began by hoping she was well. She opened it on her phone while the kettle boiled. The new figure was on the second page. She enlarged it with two fingers, then made it small again.

At work she wrote down what she earned and what she spent. There were things she could stop buying, but not enough of them. She left the paper beneath her keyboard when she went to lunch and was relieved to find it still there afterwards.

Her father rang that evening about a letter from the council. Once she had explained which number he needed to call, neither of them hung up.

“Everything all right?” he asked.

She told him about the rent.

“Well,” he said, “there’s here.”

She watched a drop of water travel down the kitchen window. On the sill, the basil plant had gone brown at the stems, though she had remembered to water it.

“Only until I find somewhere,” she said.

“Of course.”

On Saturday she gave notice. The agent replied within eleven minutes, asking whether she could accommodate viewings during working hours. She said yes, provided someone was there with them.

For the rest of the weekend she did very little. She washed a towel, bought milk, and returned a library book she had renewed three times. On Sunday night she opened the cupboard under the sink to look for bags. There were only the thin ones from the supermarket, some with holes in the corners.

---

**hop 15**

On Monday she brought home boxes from the chemist. They smelled faintly of soap, and one had a cardboard divider she spent several minutes trying to remove before deciding it might be useful for glasses.

She began with the books. Halfway along the second shelf she found a birthday card from her father with twenty pounds still folded inside. She put the money beside her keys and the card in a box without reading it again.

The letting agent had sent a list of things to clean. Light switches, extractor fan, the rubber seal around the washing machine. She printed it at work and carried it home folded into quarters. There was a space at the bottom for a forwarding address.

On Tuesday evening she rang her father to ask for his postcode.

“You know it,” he said.

“I want to make sure.”

He gave it to her slowly. In the background she heard a cupboard closing, then the tap.

“I’ve cleared the little room,” he said.

She thanked him and wrote the postcode before she forgot it.

The boxes filled more quickly once she stopped sorting things. She packed the chipped plates with the good ones and wrapped a dead battery in newspaper because it was already in her hand. By Wednesday the sitting room was empty except for the lamp she had promised the woman downstairs.

She carried it down after supper. When she came back, she reached around the door for its switch, then stood for a moment with her hand against the wall.

---

**hop 14**

By Thursday, the only things left in the kitchen were things she would need to wash. She ate standing up, using a saucepan because the bowls were wrapped, then dried it carefully and put it in the box marked winter clothes.

Her father had offered to come on Friday evening. She told him Saturday would be easier. There was nowhere for him to sit, and she didn’t want to watch him notice this.

“Early, then,” he said.

“Not too early.”

They agreed on nine.

That afternoon the woman buying the bed arrived with her brother. They took the frame apart with a tool neither of them had thought to bring; she found one in the drawer beneath the oven. The brother asked whether she wanted it back.

“Yes, please,” she said, more firmly than she meant to.

After they left, she put the tool in her handbag.

She had kept the mattress for one more night. Lying close to the floor, she could see a bottle cap underneath the radiator. It had been there long enough to collect a soft rim of dust. She considered getting up for it, then turned towards the wall.

At eight the next morning, her neighbour knocked to collect the mattress. He mentioned again that his son would only need it until Christmas.

“That’s fine,” she said.

He carried it across the landing in his socks. She held both doors open, then returned to the kitchen and unplugged the toaster. It fitted in the last box if she laid it on its side.

---

**hop 13**

On the last morning she cleaned the shelf where the toaster had been. There was a pale rectangle beneath it and a scatter of crumbs along the wall. She wiped twice, then rinsed the cloth and left it folded beside the sink. It belonged to the flat, she decided.

Her father rang from downstairs.

“There’s a space now,” he said. “If we’re quick.”

They carried the boxes without discussing which were heavy. He took one from her halfway down the stairs, and she let him, though it contained only bedding. Outside, a man in a delivery van waited while they fitted the final bag behind the passenger seat.

“Everything?” her father asked.

She went back for the charger plugged in beside the bed. Without the bed there, the socket seemed unusually low.

The journey took forty minutes. Her father had brought a flask, but neither of them wanted coffee. At a red light he asked whether she still ate mushrooms.

“Yes.”

“I wasn’t sure.”

At the house, he reversed close to the front step. The hedge had been cut back since her last visit, exposing the neighbour’s broken fence. She noticed he had mended the gate with a piece of wood that didn’t match.

Her old room had a chest of drawers in it now. He had cleared the top, leaving four darker circles where something had stood.

“Put things wherever,” he said.

She set her bag on the narrow bed. Downstairs, he was already bringing in another box. She moved the chair out of his way.

---

**hop 12**

The first box she unpacked was the one marked WINTER, though she had written KITCHEN underneath and crossed it out. She found a pair of boots wrapped in a tea towel, three mugs, and the extension lead she had spent the last week looking for.

Her father stood in the doorway with a second set of keys.

“The small one sticks,” he said. “Pull the door towards you.”

“I remember.”

“Right.” He put them on the chest of drawers.

They had agreed she would stay until she found her feet. Neither of them had said what this would involve. At first she kept the boxes closed, taking out only what she needed for work. Then she needed a warmer coat, and then the shoes for Helen’s leaving drinks. By Christmas, the empty boxes were flattened behind the wardrobe.

In February her father bought a new calendar and transferred her late shifts onto it from the old one. He had used a blue pen for hers and black for his dentist appointments and the dates the bins changed.

“You don’t need to keep track,” she said.

“It’s for the dinners.”

She looked at the small blue letters filling the squares through April. He had copied the whole rota.

The next morning, on the train, she opened a property website. There were questions about location and budget. She typed the name of the neighbourhood she used to live in, then removed it. For the rest of the journey she watched the screen go dark, waking it with her thumb whenever the train stopped.

---

**hop 11**

Mara began looking at flats during her lunch break, eating a sandwich over the keyboard. She saved the ones she could afford and a few she couldn’t, then deleted those before going home.

Her father had cleared a shelf for her in the bathroom when she moved back. That had been in November. By March, her things had spread to the windowsill, and he had put his shaving brush in the cabinet without mentioning it.

There was nothing wrong with living there. This was what she told people at work, and it was true. The house was warm. Her father cooked on Tuesdays and Thursdays, the days she got in late. If she bought milk on her way home, there was usually an unopened bottle already in the fridge.

One Saturday she came downstairs to find her washing folded on the kitchen table. Her pants were stacked between two towels.

“You didn’t have to do that,” she said.

“It was dry.”

She carried it upstairs and put it away. Then she sat on the bed and called an estate agent.

That evening she told her father she was looking for somewhere.

“To buy?”

“To rent.”

He nodded and moved his plate so she could reach the salt.

For several days he asked nothing. She wondered whether he was waiting for her to change her mind. Then, while she was making tea, he left a small notebook beside the kettle.

“For measurements,” he said.

She opened it. The first page had the width of her chest of drawers written on it.

---

**hop 10**

The first flat she went to see had a washing machine in the bathroom and a view of the railway cutting. Her father came with her, although she had only asked him for a lift. He opened the cupboard under the sink while the agent was explaining the deposit.

“What are you looking for?” Mara asked afterwards.

“Signs of water.”

“It’s under a sink.”

He waited until they were in the car before saying anything else.

She went to the second viewing alone. This flat had narrow stairs and a kitchen with space for a table if she bought a small one. The previous tenant had left a calendar behind the door. It was still turned to April, with a dentist’s appointment written in a square.

Mara paid the holding deposit that afternoon.

At home, her father asked about the boiler. She said it was white and on the wall. He gave a short laugh, then asked whether there had been a certificate.

“Yes,” she said, though she could not remember.

For the next fortnight she packed in the evenings. She used boxes from the supermarket, choosing the ones that had held crisps because they were clean. Her father brought packing tape home on Wednesday and a second roll on Friday.

“The first one’s not finished,” she told him.

“It will be.”

On the morning of the move, she found him beside the car, rearranging the boot. He had put an old blanket over the folded seats. Her boxes stood along the hall, leaving just enough room for them to pass sideways.

---

**hop 9**

When Mara moved into the flat, her father gave her a box of things he thought she would need. There were spare bulbs, a torch without batteries, two extension leads and a packet of hooks for the backs of doors. He had also included a small spirit level, wrapped in one of his old socks.

“You can keep that,” he said when she took it out.

She put the box in the cupboard beneath the stairs. The flat was upstairs, but the cupboard belonged to her; the letting agent had made a point of showing her the separate key.

Her father carried in the last of the books and stood looking at the empty wall above the fireplace.

“Shelves would go there.”

“I'm not sure we're allowed to drill.”

“You could ask.”

She said she would, though she had already bought a low bookcase that was due to arrive on Thursday. He pressed his thumb against a patch of raised wallpaper, then took his coat from the banister.

For the first few weeks he rang on Sundays to ask how she was getting on. She told him about the buses and the woman downstairs who took in parcels. Sometimes he asked whether she had used anything from the box.

“The extension lead,” she said once.

“Which one?”

“The white one.”

He seemed pleased with this. He told her not to run it beneath a rug, even if the wire was in the way.

In January she bought batteries for the torch. She left it on the kitchen windowsill, where she would remember it if the lights went out.

---

**hop 8**

The kitchen tap had begun to drip in February. Mara put a folded cloth beneath it at night, which silenced it until the cloth became soaked. In the mornings she wrung it out and draped it over the divider between the sinks.

Her father noticed during a visit in March. He had brought six eggs in a margarine tub, each separated from the others by a twist of newspaper. A man at the allotments had given him too many.

“You want to get that seen to,” he said.

“I've told the landlord.”

He turned the tap firmly, waited, then turned it back a fraction. A drop gathered and fell.

“Washer,” he said.

She made tea while he went down to his car. From the window she watched him search the boot, lifting a shopping bag and setting it carefully on the pavement. He came back with a spanner but couldn't get the tap apart. He said he hadn't brought the right screwdriver.

“Leave it,” she said. “Really. They'll send someone.”

He stayed for a second cup. Before leaving, he washed the margarine tub and dried it with a paper towel. The spanner remained beside the draining board. Mara noticed it after his car had pulled away, but didn't call him back.

By April the landlord's plumber had fitted a new tap. It was taller than the old one, and water splashed onto her sleeves when she rinsed a plate. She meant to mention this to her father. He would have had something to say about it.

---

**hop 7**

Her father had telephoned on Sunday to ask whether she still had the small adjustable spanner. She said she would look, though she knew it was under her sink, holding down the corner of a curled shelf liner.

“No hurry,” he said. Then he asked if she was eating properly, in the tone he used for checking tyre pressures.

On Tuesday a neighbour called from his house. Mara could hear the kitchen clock behind the woman’s voice. It seemed unreasonable that the neighbour should be standing close enough to hear it too.

At the hospital, a nurse gave her his watch in a transparent bag. The strap had kept the shape of his wrist. There were also some coins and his bus pass, its photograph showing him in the expression he reserved for people who told him where to stand.

She asked whether he had come in wearing a coat.

The nurse checked a cupboard, then another cupboard. Mara wished she had not asked.

Her brother rang from Perth while she was waiting for a taxi. He said he would try to get a flight. She told him not to spend foolish money, and they were both quiet, recognising whose instruction that had been.

At home she retrieved the spanner and put it in her handbag. In the morning she carried it to the registrar’s, where the clerk needed a date she could not remember. Looking for her notebook, she laid the spanner on his desk. He moved it aside to make room for the certificate, and she thanked him.

---

**hop 6**

The woman at the funeral office asked whether her father had expressed any wishes. Mara said he had wanted his front gate rehung so it opened towards the house, then apologised. The woman waited with her pen above the form.

“Not that I know of,” Mara said.

She chose the middle-priced coffin because the cheapest had handles that looked difficult to hold. Afterwards she understood that she would not be expected to hold them.

Her father’s coat remained on the hook behind his kitchen door. In one pocket she found a folded receipt for washers; in the other, a paper bag containing two peppermints stuck together. She put both things back. His keys she took, although she already had a set and there was nobody left to lock out.

On the morning of the funeral, the harbour was hidden by rain. Men arrived wearing shoes she had never seen them in. They stood beneath the crematorium porch and shook water from their sleeves, speaking quietly about where they had parked.

Someone told her that her father had been a good man to work beside. She wanted to ask what he had been like on an ordinary Tuesday, but another hand was already being offered.

Back at the flat, she discovered that she had brought home the envelope containing the minister’s fee. She telephoned the office and promised to send it on.

Before going to bed, she opened her door to check the stairs. A wet leaf lay on the landing. She picked it up and left the light burning.

---

**hop 5**

For three weeks after the funeral, Mara left the landing light on. Her father had never visited the flat after dark, and in the last year had not visited at all, but she kept finding reasons for it. The stairs were steep. The switch downstairs was unreliable. Her neighbour sometimes came home late from the boats.

Then the bulb went and she did not replace it.

There were other things she managed by letting them stop. The milk order. The Saturday call to the harbour office, where someone would fetch her father if he was passing or say they had seen him earlier. She had never known what to ask once he came to the telephone. He reported on the weather across the water as though she lived much further away.

“Same here,” she usually said.

His last message concerned a saucepan. He thought she had taken the small one with the heavy lid when she moved, and wanted to know whether she still used it. She had listened twice, standing beside the cooker. The saucepan was in the cupboard under the sink, full of clothes pegs.

She emptied it, washed it, and left it on the draining board. For several days she put things beside it rather than move it back.

When the postman knocked, she was drying the lid with a tea towel. He had brought a packet too thick for the letterbox and a catalogue addressed to the previous tenant. She signed on his machine with her finger. He waited while she tried a second time.

---

**hop 4**

The letter had arrived folded around a smaller envelope, the sort her father used for keeping screws. On the front, in his handwriting, was the word Kitchen. Nothing inside.

Mara shook it over the table, then held it against the window. A square of cleaner paper showed where a key had lain.

The authority’s letter explained that the house would be cleared on Monday. Personal effects could be collected before then by arrangement. Everything else would be disposed of. Someone had corrected the telephone number in black biro, pressing hard enough to dent the sheet beneath.

She rang on Thursday. A woman asked whether there was furniture.

“I don’t know what’s left.”

“Well, nothing large can go in the launch.”

On Friday night, Mara put newspaper, string and a pair of scissors in her bag. She added her father’s envelope, although there was no reason to take it back. The kitchen lock had probably been changed with all the others.

Her neighbour was already outside when she went for the cart in the morning. He tightened the strap around her boxes and asked if she wanted company.

“It’s mostly sorting,” she said.

He showed her how to lift the bad wheel over a kerb.

Downhill, the cart kept catching her heels. Shops were opening along the quay, and a girl in a butcher’s apron sluiced yesterday’s sawdust into the gutter. Mara stopped before the harbour office to let a delivery van reverse. Through the open doorway she could see a man searching a board of keys.

---

**hop 3**

At the harbour office, Mara signed for a key and a sheet of tide times. The clerk had circled the last safe crossing in blue ink.

“Half past four,” he said.

“The boat’s collecting me at five.”

He took the sheet back, considered it, and returned it without making another mark.

Outside, the handcart rattled over the cobbles. She had borrowed it from a neighbour who used it for crates of apples; one wheel dragged, and the frame smelled faintly of cider. Empty cardboard boxes were strapped across it. She had brought six, which seemed both excessive and insufficient.

The boatman was waiting below the fish market. He looked older than she remembered, though she had last seen him at the funeral only eight months before. He watched her collapse the cart and fit it beneath the thwart.

“Just you?”

“Just me.”

He untied the boat.

Beyond the breakwater, the lighthouse appeared in pieces: first the lantern above the ridge, then the white tower, then the low house crouched beside it. The windows caught no sunlight. As a child, she had been able to tell which room her father occupied by the colour of the glass.

Halfway across, the boatman stopped the engine and cleared weed from the propeller with a knife. In the sudden quiet, Mara heard a thin metallic knocking from the island.

“Loose gutter,” she said.

He wiped the knife on his trousers.

At the landing, the bottom step was underwater. Mara stood and pulled her bag free, waiting for him to take the other end of the cart.

---

**hop 2**

The boatman would not help her unload.

He held the bow against the landing with an oar while Mara lifted the folded handcart onto the steps. When she reached for the rope, he drew it back.

“I’ll wait on the western side,” he said. “Water’s deeper there.”

“You used to come right up to the door.”

“That was when your father kept the light.”

She almost reminded him that the lamp had been automated long before her father died, but he was already pushing away.

The lighthouse key still wore its red wool thread. Her father had tied it there so she could find it in the shingle if she dropped it. She had been nine then, old enough to resent the precaution and young enough to need it twice.

Inside, the kitchen smelled of wet plaster. Someone had turned all four chairs toward the window.

Mara set her bag down without touching them.

She had allowed herself one afternoon. The demolition surveyors were coming on Monday, and anything left behind would go with the building. That was what the letter said: with the building, as though the lighthouse were departing under its own power.

She began upstairs, opening cupboards swollen shut by salt. Sheets tore when she lifted them. A box of photographs had become a single black-edged block.

In her old bedroom, she found the small rubber boots her father had refused to throw away. Sand spilled from one heel, although she remembered washing them before they left.

She put them back and went to fetch the telescope.

---

**hop 1**

By four o’clock, Mara had carried everything worth saving down from the lighthouse: the brass telescope, her father’s logbooks, and a biscuit tin containing three teeth, a wedding ring, and the key to a house that no longer existed.

The boat waited beyond the causeway. From the kitchen window it looked close enough to touch, though she knew the walk would take twenty minutes with the handcart, longer if its bad wheel caught between the stones.

She checked the tide table again. Six seventeen. Plenty of time.

Upstairs, something scraped across the lantern-room floor.

Mara stood very still, one hand on the tin. The lighthouse had been empty for eleven years before she returned that morning. She had found gulls in the stairwell and a dead rat behind the stove. Neither explained the sound of a chair being pulled out carefully, then pushed back in.

“Not today,” she said.

Her father’s last logbook lay open on the table. Most entries recorded wind, visibility, passing vessels. The final page contained only a date and a sentence she had avoided reading twice.

If she comes back, keep her above the water.

Outside, the boatman sounded his horn.

Mara shut the book and tucked it beneath the others. She would ask him about the sentence once they reached the mainland. He had known her father before the drinking, before the nightly walks along the causeway with a lantern held low.

She dragged the cart through the doorway.

At the first bend, where dry stone should have shone white, something silver moved.

---

**seed**

The tide came in an hour early.
